- Important space agencies, organizations, and companies with relevant information (unverified) -
Name Establishment Year Type Key Rockets/Vehicles Notable Missions (2020-2025, with historical context where relevant)
Government Agency
National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA) 1958 Government Agency
  • Space Launch System (SLS): Super heavy-lift, up to 95 t to LEO, human-rated for deep space.
  • Orion: Crew capsule for lunar/Mars transit, supports Artemis program.
  • Saturn V: (historical, 1967-1973) Heavy-lift, 140 t to LEO, used for Apollo Moon landings.
  • Apollo 11: (1969, historical) First human Moon landing.
  • Artemis I: (2022) Uncrewed lunar orbit test of SLS/Orion.
  • Europa Clipper: (2024) Jupiter icy moon flyby mission.
  • SPHEREx: (2025) All-sky infrared survey telescope.
  • CHAPEA: (2024) 378-day Mars habitat analog.
Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) 1969 Government Agency
  • LVM3 (GSLV Mk III): Heavy-lift, up to 8 t to GTO, reusable tech in development.
  • PSLV: Medium-lift, up to 1.75 t to SSO, highly reliable for small sats.
  • SSLV: Small-lift, up to 500 kg to LEO, rapid deployment.
  • Chandrayaan-3: (2023) Successful lunar south pole landing.
  • Aditya-L1: (2023) Solar observatory at L1 point.
  • SpaDeX: (2024) Space docking demonstration.
  • NISAR: (2025) Dual-frequency radar Earth imaging with NASA.
China National Space Administration (CNSA) 1993 Government Agency
  • Long March 5: Heavy-lift, up to 25 t to LEO, supports lunar/planetary missions.
  • Long March 7: Medium-lift, up to 13.5 t to LEO, for space station modules.
  • Mengtian/Mengzhou: Crew and cargo spacecraft for Tiangong station.
  • Tianwen-1: (2020) Mars orbiter, lander, and rover mission.
  • Chang’e-5: (2020) Lunar sample return mission.
  • Tiangong space station: (2021-2022) Fully operational with 3 modules.
  • Chang’e-6: (2024) Lunar far-side sample return.
Roscosmos 1992 Government Agency
  • Soyuz-2: Medium-lift, up to 7.8 t to LEO, reliable for crew/cargo.
  • Angara A5: Heavy-lift, up to 24.5 t to LEO, modular design.
  • Progress: Cargo spacecraft for ISS resupply.
  • Luna-25: (2023) Lunar south pole lander (failed to land).
  • ISS missions: (2020-2025) Continuous crew and cargo support.
  • Orel: (2025) Uncrewed orbital flight for new crew vehicle.
  • ExoMars: (2022) Rover landing assistance (canceled).
Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA) 2003 Government Agency
  • H3: Medium-lift, up to 6.5 t to GTO, reusable tech in trials.
  • Epsilon: Small-lift, up to 1.2 t to LEO, for CubeSats.
  • Hayabusa2: (2020-2021) Ryugu asteroid sample return.
  • MMX: (2024) Martian moons sample return mission prep.
  • SLIM: (2023) Precision lunar landing success.
  • XRISM: (2023) X-ray imaging spectroscopy mission.
Space Agency of the Republic of Azerbaijan (Azercosmos) 2010 Government Agency
  • No indigenous rockets; uses commercial launch providers (e.g., Ariane, Falcon).
  • Azerspace satellites: Telecom and Earth observation platforms.
  • Azerspace-2: (2020) Telecom satellite for regional connectivity.
  • Azersky-2: (2023) High-resolution Earth observation satellite.
  • Regional EO network: (2024) Expanded monitoring capabilities.
United Arab Emirates Space Agency (UAESA) 2014 Government Agency
  • No indigenous rockets; uses JAXA, SpaceX for launches.
  • Hope Probe: Mars orbiter for atmospheric studies.
  • MBZ-SAT: High-resolution imaging satellite.
  • Hope Probe: (2020) First UAE Mars mission, atmospheric data collection.
  • MBZ-SAT: (2024) Advanced Earth observation satellite launch.
  • Lunar rover: (2025) First UAE lunar mission with ispace.
Intergovernmental Organization
European Space Agency (ESA) 1975 Intergovernmental Organization
  • Ariane 6: Heavy-lift, up to 21.6 t to GTO, flexible configurations (A62/A64).
  • Vega-C: Small-lift, up to 2.3 t to LEO, for small satellites.
  • Juice: (2023) Jupiter icy moons explorer mission.
  • Euclid: (2023) Dark energy and universe mapping telescope at L2.
  • Ariane 6: (2024) Successful heavy-lift debut.
  • Proba-3: (2024) Precision formation-flying solar observatory.
Private Company
SpaceX 2002 Private Company
  • Falcon 9: Reusable medium-lift, up to 22.8 t to LEO, 450+ successful landings.
  • Falcon Heavy: Heavy-lift, up to 63.8 t to LEO, triple-booster reusability.
  • Starship: Super heavy-lift, up to 150 t to LEO, fully reusable, 10 test flights by 2025.
  • Starlink: (2020-2025) 5,000+ satellites for global broadband.
  • Crew Dragon Demo-2: (2020) First private crewed ISS mission.
  • Artemis HLS: (2025) Uncrewed lunar landing demo.
  • Fram2: (2025) First crewed polar retrograde orbit.
Blue Origin 2000 Private Company
  • New Shepard: Suborbital, up to 6 passengers to 100 km, 20+ flights, fully reusable.
  • New Glenn: Heavy-lift, up to 45 t to LEO, reusable first stage, maiden flight 2025.
  • Blue Moon: Lunar lander for cargo/crew, supports Artemis.
  • NS-25: (2024) Suborbital crewed flight with research payloads.
  • New Glenn: (2025) Orbital test with ESCAPADE Mars probes.
  • Blue Moon Mk1: (2025) Uncrewed lunar cargo delivery.
  • NS-31: (2025) All-female celebrity suborbital mission.
United Launch Alliance (ULA) 2006 Private Company
  • Atlas V: Medium-lift, up to 18.8 t to LEO, high reliability.
  • Vulcan Centaur: Heavy-lift, up to 27 t to LEO, reusable engines, debuted 2024.
  • Perseverance: (2020) Mars rover launch for NASA.
  • Vulcan: (2024) Successful orbital debut.
  • Sierra Nevada Dream Chaser: (2024) ISS cargo mission.
  • Amazon Kuiper: (2025) Broadband satellite constellation launches.
Rocket Lab 2006 Private Company
  • Electron: Small-lift, up to 300 kg to LEO, 50+ launches by 2025.
  • Neutron: Medium-lift, up to 15 t to LEO, reusable, first flight 2025.
  • CAPSTONE: (2022) Lunar CubeSat for Artemis navigation.
  • Venus mission: (2023) First private Venus probe.
  • Neutron: (2025) Medium-lift rocket debut.
  • Kinetic Mining: (2024) Asteroid resource survey.
Arianespace 1980 Private Company
  • Ariane 5: Heavy-lift, up to 20 t to LEO, retired 2023.
  • Ariane 6: Heavy-lift, up to 21.6 t to GTO, operational 2024.
  • Vega-C: Small-lift, up to 2.3 t to LEO.
  • James Webb Space Telescope: (2021) Launched on Ariane 5.
  • Ariane 6: (2024) Successful heavy-lift debut.
  • Galileo satellites: (2020-2025) Navigation constellation expansion.
  • OneWeb: (2022-2023) Broadband satellite deployments.
Northrop Grumman 1994 (space division) Private Company
  • Antares: Medium-lift, up to 8 t to LEO, ISS resupply.
  • Cygnus: Cargo spacecraft for ISS missions.
  • Minotaur: Small-lift, up to 1.7 t to LEO, for government payloads.
  • Cygnus NG-14 to NG-20: (2020-2024) ISS resupply missions.
  • Antares 230+: (2022) Improved payload capacity.
  • Minotaur IV: (2023) DoD satellite launch.
  • Artemis: (2024) Lunar mission component supply.
Space Systems/Loral (SSL, now Maxar) 1957 (space division) Private Company
  • No rockets; builds satellites launched by commercial providers.
  • Maxar satellites: High-resolution imaging and telecom platforms.
  • WorldView Legion: (2021-2024) Six imaging satellites deployed.
  • Intelsat 40e: (2023) Telecom satellite with NASA TEMPO payload.
  • Artemis PPE: (2024) Power and propulsion for lunar Gateway.
ispace 2010 Private Company
  • HAKUTO-R: Lunar lander, up to 30 kg payload, commercial delivery.
  • No rockets; uses SpaceX Falcon 9 for launches.
  • HAKUTO-R M1: (2022) Lunar landing attempt (failed).
  • HAKUTO-R M2: (2024) Second lunar landing with micro-rover.
  • UAESA rover: (2025) Lunar mission for UAE Space Agency.
Relativity Space 2015 Private Company
  • Terran 1: Small-lift, up to 1.25 t to LEO, 3D-printed, retired 2023.
  • Terran R: Medium-lift, up to 23.5 t to LEO, reusable, first flight 2025.
  • Terran 1 GLHF: (2023) First 3D-printed rocket launch attempt.
  • Terran R: (2024) Ground tests for reusable rocket.
  • Commercial contracts: (2025) Intelsat, Iridium satellite launches.
- Important space missions (unverified) -
Organization/Agency Key Missions
Government Agencies
National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA)
  • Apollo 11: (1969) First human Moon landing; Armstrong and Aldrin walked on lunar surface, returned 21.5 kg of samples.
  • Voyager 1 & 2: (1977) Interstellar probes; explored Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Neptune, now in interstellar space, still transmitting.
  • Hubble Space Telescope: (1990) Orbital observatory; revolutionized astronomy with deep-space imaging, operational 35+ years.
  • Deep Impact: (2005) Comet impactor; struck Tempel 1, revealed porous structure and organics; extended as EPOXI for Hartley 2 flyby (2010).
  • Kepler: (2009) Exoplanet hunter; confirmed 2,700+ exoplanets, transformed understanding of planetary systems.
  • Curiosity Rover: (2012) Mars exploration; confirmed ancient water flows, still active in Gale Crater after 13 years.
  • OSIRIS-REx: (2016-2023) Asteroid sample return; collected 121.6 g from Bennu, delivered to Earth in 2023.
  • Perseverance Rover: (2020) Mars sample collection; landed in Jezero Crater, caching samples for future return.
  • DART: (2022) Planetary defense test; impacted Dimorphos, shortened orbit by 32 minutes, first deflection success.
  • Artemis I: (2022) Uncrewed lunar orbit; tested SLS/Orion for Artemis program, enabling crewed landings.
  • Psyche: (2023) Asteroid orbiter; studying metal-rich Psyche asteroid, launched October 2023, en route.
  • Europa Clipper: (2024) Jupiter moon flyby; studying Europa’s ocean for habitability, arriving 2030.
  • IMAP: (2025) Heliosphere boundary probe; launched September 2025 to map solar wind interactions.
Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO)
  • Chandrayaan-1: (2008) Lunar orbiter; confirmed water molecules on Moon, mapped surface globally.
  • Mangalyaan: (2013) Mars orbiter; first Indian interplanetary mission, studied atmosphere for 8+ years.
  • Chandrayaan-2: (2019) Lunar orbiter and lander; orbiter mapped Moon, lander crashed, led to Chandrayaan-3.
  • Chandrayaan-3: (2023) Lunar south pole landing; Pragyan rover confirmed sulfur, operated 14 days.
  • Aditya-L1: (2023) Solar observatory at L1; studying corona, solar flares, and space weather.
  • SpaDeX: (2024) Space docking demo; tested orbital rendezvous for future human missions.
  • NISAR: (2025) Earth radar imaging with NASA; monitors ecosystems, disasters, launched July 2025.
China National Space Administration (CNSA)
  • Chang’e-1: (2007) Lunar orbiter; mapped Moon in 3D, first Chinese lunar mission.
  • Chang’e-3: (2013) Lunar rover Yutu; first Chinese soft landing, studied regolith for 2+ years.
  • Tianwen-1: (2020) Mars orbiter, lander, rover; Zhurong explored Utopia Planitia, operated 347 days.
  • Chang’e-5: (2020) Lunar sample return; retrieved 1.7 kg of Moon rocks, youngest samples studied.
  • Tiangong Space Station: (2021-2022) Modular assembly; fully operational, hosted taikonauts, international crews.
  • Chang’e-6: (2024) Far-side lunar sample return; collected 2 kg from South Pole-Aitken basin.
  • Tianwen-2: (2025) Asteroid sample return; en route to Kamo’oalewa, halfway milestone October 2025.
Roscosmos State Corporation
  • Sputnik 1: (1957) First artificial satellite; launched space age, orbited for 3 months.
  • Luna 9: (1966) First lunar soft landing; transmitted surface images, proved Moon’s firmness.
  • Venera 7: (1970) First Venus surface landing; transmitted data for 23 minutes despite harsh conditions.
  • Mir Space Station: (1986-2001) First modular space station; hosted long-duration crews, international experiments.
  • ISS Missions: (2020-2025) Continuous crew and cargo via Soyuz/Progress, supporting station operations.
  • Luna-25: (2023) Lunar south pole lander; crashed on landing, marked return to lunar exploration.
Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA)
  • Hayabusa: (2003) Asteroid sample return; retrieved 1 mg from Itokawa, first of its kind.
  • Hayabusa2: (2014-2020) Ryugu asteroid sample return; delivered 5.4 g, studied organic compounds.
  • AKARI: (2006) Infrared telescope; mapped 90% of sky, discovered new star-forming regions.
  • SLIM: (2023) Precision lunar landing; achieved 100-m accuracy, first Japanese lunar success.
  • XRISM: (2023) X-ray imaging spectroscopy; mapping high-energy cosmic phenomena.
  • MMX: (2024) Martian moons mission; launched to sample Phobos, return planned for 2029.
United Arab Emirates Space Agency (UAESA)
  • Hope Probe: (2020) First UAE Mars mission; orbiter studying atmosphere, delivering weather data.
  • MBZ-SAT: (2024) High-resolution Earth observation satellite; supports urban planning, disaster monitoring.
  • Rashid II: (2025) First UAE lunar rover; launched via ispace, exploring lunar surface.
Intergovernmental Organization
European Space Agency (ESA)
  • Huygens: (2005) Titan lander with NASA; first landing on Saturn’s moon, studied methane lakes.
  • Rosetta: (2004-2016) Comet orbiter and lander; Philae landed on 67P/Churyumov-Gerasimenko, analyzed comet composition.
  • Gaia: (2013) Stellar mapping; cataloged 1.8B+ stars, refining galactic structure models.
  • BepiColombo: (2018) Mercury orbiter with JAXA; studying planet’s surface, magnetic field, arriving 2025.
  • Juice: (2023) Jupiter icy moons explorer; studying Ganymede, Callisto, Europa, arriving 2031.
  • Euclid: (2023) Dark energy telescope; mapping universe at L2, refining cosmology models.
Private Companies
SpaceX
  • Falcon 1: (2008) First private orbital rocket; reached orbit, proved commercial launch viability.
  • Crew Dragon Demo-2: (2020) First private crewed ISS mission; docked with 2 astronauts, certified for regular flights.
  • Starlink: (2020-2025) 5,000+ satellites deployed; global broadband network, 2M+ users by 2025.
  • Inspiration4: (2021) First all-civilian orbital mission; 3-day flight, raised $240M for charity.
  • Artemis HLS: (2025) Uncrewed lunar landing demo; Starship selected for Artemis III crewed landing.
  • Fram2: (2025) First crewed polar retrograde orbit; studied Earth’s poles, auroras.
Blue Origin
  • NS-16: (2021) First crewed New Shepard flight; carried Jeff Bezos, 3 others to 100 km, suborbital success.
  • NS-25: (2024) Suborbital flight with research payloads; advanced microgravity experiments.
  • New Glenn Inaugural: (2025) Orbital test; carried ESCAPADE Mars probes, marked heavy-lift debut.
  • Blue Moon Mk1: (2025) Uncrewed lunar cargo delivery; supports Artemis program logistics.
Rocket Lab
  • CAPSTONE: (2022) Lunar CubeSat; tested navigation for Artemis, orbited Moon’s rectilinear halo.
  • Venus Mission: (2023) First private Venus probe; studied atmosphere, proof of smallsat capability.
  • Kinetic Mining: (2024) Asteroid resource survey; tested in-orbit prospecting tech.
  • Neutron Test: (2025) Medium-lift rocket debut; carried commercial payloads to LEO.
ispace
  • HAKUTO-R M1: (2022) Lunar landing attempt; crashed but first private lunar effort.
  • HAKUTO-R M2: (2024) Lunar landing with micro-rover; delivered payloads, partial success.
  • UAESA Rover: (2025) Lunar mission for UAE; Rashid II rover exploring lunar surface.
- Important Indian satellites (unverified) -
Series/Type Key Satellites
Early/Experimental Satellites
Early/Experimental (Foundational satellites for technology demonstration)
  • Aryabhata: (1975) India’s first satellite; launched by USSR, carried X-ray astronomy, aeronomy experiments; active 4 days, marked ISRO’s entry.
  • Bhaskara-I: (1979) First remote sensing test; TV camera, microwave radiometer; studied land/ocean, launched by USSR.
  • Rohini-1B: (1980) First satellite launched by Indian SLV-3; carried imaging payload, orbited 10 months, proved indigenous launch capability.
  • APPLE: (1981) First GEO communication test; Ariane-launched, enabled TV/radio relay experiments, operational 2 years.
Communication Satellites (INSAT/GSAT Series)
INSAT (Indian National Satellite System: Multipurpose GEO satellites for telecom, broadcasting, meteorology)
  • INSAT-1B: (1983) First operational INSAT; enabled national TV broadcasting, telecom; operational 8 years, at 74°E.
  • INSAT-3A: (2003) C/Ku-band, meteorological imager; supported rural connectivity, weather forecasting; co-located at 93.5°E.
  • INSAT-3D: (2013) Advanced meteorological satellite; 6-channel imager/sounder for weather, disaster warning; at 82°E.
  • INSAT-4CR: (2007) Extended C-band; mobile communications, remote area connectivity; at 74°E, 12-year life.
GSAT (Geosynchronous Satellite: Advanced communication satellites augmenting INSAT)
  • GSAT-2: (2003) First GSLV-launched; C/Ku-band for TV/radio, MSS for mobile services; at 48°E, 8-year life.
  • GSAT-7/INSAT-4F: (2013) Military comms; X/Ku/UHF bands for navy/air force, secure voice/data over Indian Ocean.
  • GSAT-9: (South Asia Satellite, 2017) SAARC initiative; Ku/Ka/C-bands for regional telemedicine, education; at 48°E.
  • GSAT-30: (2020) High-throughput; 12 Ku/C-bands for broadband, DTH; replaces INSAT-4A, at 83°E, 15-year life.
  • GSAT-31: (2019) High-power Ku-band; supports GAGAN navigation, communication for India/Islands; at 48°E.
Earth Observation/Remote Sensing Satellites (IRS Series)
IRS (Indian Remote Sensing: Sun-synchronous orbit for agriculture, disaster management, mapping)
  • IRS-1A: (1988) First operational IRS; LISS-I camera for land use, agriculture; 70m resolution, foundational for EO.
  • IRS-1C: (1995) Panchromatic/multispectral; 5.8m resolution for urban planning, stereo mapping; global impact.
  • Resourcesat-1/IRS-P6: (2003) AWiFS for wide-area monitoring (56m); agriculture, forestry, water resource management.
  • Cartosat-2: (2007) Sub-meter resolution; defense, urban mapping; high-precision cartography, 0.8m imagery.
  • EOS-01: (2020) Advanced SAR; all-weather imaging, disaster monitoring, continues IRS legacy; launched by PSLV-C49.
Oceanography Satellites (Oceansat Series)
Oceansat (Ocean color monitoring, sea surface studies)
  • Oceansat-1/IRS-P4: (1999) Ocean Colour Monitor (OCM); phytoplankton mapping, fisheries support; Ku-band scatterometer for winds.
  • Oceansat-2: (2009) Enhanced OCM/Scatterometer; ocean productivity, cyclone tracking; 360m resolution, 5+ years life.
  • EOS-08/Oceansat-3A: (2024) Advanced OCM, thermal IR; ocean health, coastal monitoring; launched August 2024 by SSLV.
Meteorological Satellites (METSAT/Kalpana Series)
METSAT/Kalpana (Dedicated GEO meteorological satellites)
  • Kalpana-1: (METSAT-1, 2002) First dedicated metsat; VHRR for cloud motion, rainfall; renamed after Kalpana Chawla, at 74°E.
  • INSAT-3DR: (2016) 6-channel imager/sounder; 3D rainfall maps, cyclone tracking; augments INSAT-3D, at 74°E.
Navigation Satellites (IRNSS/NavIC)
NavIC (Navigation with Indian Constellation: Regional satellite navigation)
  • IRNSS-1A: (2013) First of 7-satellite constellation; L5/S-band for positioning, <20m accuracy over India/region.
  • NavIC Full Constellation: (2018) 7 satellites operational; supports GNSS augmentation, disaster alerts, maritime navigation.
Radar Imaging Satellites (RISAT Series)
RISAT (Radar Imaging Satellite: All-weather Earth observation)
  • RISAT-1: (2012) C-band SAR; 1-3m resolution for agriculture, forestry in clouds/rain; launched by PSLV-C19.
  • RISAT-2B: (2019) X-band SAR; disaster management, border surveillance; enhanced resolution, multiple launches till 2020.
Scientific/Technology Demonstration Satellites
Scientific/Tech Demo (Specialized missions for science and technology)
  • SROSS-C2: (1994) Stretched Rohini; gamma-ray burst detection, atmospheric studies; operational 3 years.
  • ScatSat-1: (2016) Ku-band scatterometer; global ocean wind mapping, cyclone prediction; complements Oceansat, 2-year life.
  • CMS-01: (2020) Extended C-band; communication services for remote areas, replaces GSAT-12; at 83°E.
  • EOS-06: (2022) Multispectral/thermal imaging; ocean and land monitoring, continues Oceansat legacy; launched by PSLV-C54.
- Important medical technologies (unverified) -
Technology Key Applications/Advancements
Assisted Reproductive Technologies
In-Vitro Fertilization (IVF)
  • Combines eggs/sperm in lab for embryo implantation; treats infertility, used in ~5% of global infertility cases.
  • First success 1978; ~8M babies born by 2025; success rates ~20-35% per cycle (women under 35).
  • Advancements: Preimplantation genetic testing (PGT) screens for disorders; AI embryo selection boosts success by 10-15% (2025).
Three-Parent IVF (Mitochondrial Replacement Therapy - MRT)
  • Replaces faulty maternal mitochondria with donor’s to prevent mitochondrial diseases; uses pronuclear/spindle transfer.
  • UK-approved 2015; ~10 healthy births by 2025, mutation risk <2% (Newcastle data).
  • Applications: Treats Leigh syndrome; 2025 trials explore infertility benefits; ethical debates on germline modification.
Intracytoplasmic Sperm Injection (ICSI)
  • Injects single sperm into egg during IVF; addresses male infertility (low sperm count/motility).
  • Introduced 1992; used in ~60% of IVF cycles by 2025; success rates 30-40%.
  • Advancements: Microfluidic sperm sorting; robotic ICSI systems improve precision (2024 trials).
Gamete Intrafallopian Transfer (GIFT)
  • Transfers eggs/sperm to fallopian tubes for natural fertilization; alternative to IVF for specific infertility cases.
  • Introduced 1984; less common (~1% of ART by 2025) but effective for tubal issues; 25-30% success rate.
  • Advancements: Laparoscopic precision; combined with PGT for genetic screening in 2025.
Genetic Engineering and Cloning
CRISPR-Cas9 Gene Editing
  • Precise DNA editing for diseases like sickle cell (SCD) and beta-thalassemia; Cas9 cuts target DNA.
  • FDA-approved Casgevy (2023) for SCD/TDT; ~90% cure rate in trials; 2025 in vivo trials for muscular dystrophy.
  • Advancements: Base editing reduces off-target effects by 10x; multiplex editing targets multiple genes.
Somatic Cell Nuclear Transfer (SCNT)
  • Transfers somatic cell nucleus to enucleated egg for cloning/stem cells; reprograms to totipotent state.
  • Dolly the sheep (1996); therapeutic cloning produces patient-specific stem cells for disease modeling.
  • 2025: Human iPSC trials for Parkinson’s; primate cloning efficiency up to 5%; reproductive cloning banned.
RNA Interference (RNAi)
  • Silences genes via siRNA/miRNA; treats liver diseases like ATTR amyloidosis (patisiran, 2018).
  • 6 FDA-approved drugs by 2025 (e.g., inclisiran for cholesterol); targets cancer, HIV in trials.
  • Advancements: AI-designed siRNAs reduce off-target effects to <5%; nanoparticle delivery for CNS.
Recombinant DNA Technology
  • Produces proteins via gene insertion into hosts (e.g., bacteria); human insulin (1982) via E. coli.
  • Applications: Vaccines (HPV, hepatitis B), monoclonal antibodies; supports 50% of biologics market.
  • 2025: Synthetic biology for custom proteins; mRNA vaccine platforms built on rDNA tech.
DNA Fingerprinting (Profiling)
  • STR analysis for identification; used in forensics, paternity, transplant matching.
  • Exonerated 400+ innocents in U.S. by 2025; CODIS matches 500,000+ profiles globally.
  • 2025: Rapid sequencing (1-hour profiling); guides personalized medicine via pharmacogenomics.
Gene Therapy
  • Delivers functional genes via viral vectors (e.g., AAV) to treat genetic disorders like SMA, hemophilia.
  • 10+ FDA-approved therapies by 2025 (e.g., Zolgensma for SMA); 80% symptom reduction in trials.
  • Advancements: Non-viral vectors (nanoparticles); 2025 trials target retinal diseases, heart failure.
CAR-T Cell Therapy
  • Genetically modifies T-cells to target cancer; approved for leukemia, lymphoma (e.g., Kymriah, 2017).
  • 2025: 6 FDA approvals; ~50% remission in B-cell ALL; trials for solid tumors (lung, pancreatic).
  • Advancements: Off-the-shelf CAR-T reduces costs; CRISPR integration improves specificity.
Stem Cell Therapy
  • Uses pluripotent/adult stem cells for tissue regeneration; treats leukemia, spinal injuries.
  • HSCT for blood cancers (~50,000 transplants/year); 2025 iPSC trials for Parkinson’s, heart repair.
  • Advancements: CRISPR-edited stem cells reduce tumor risk to <1%; automated differentiation protocols.
Transplant Technologies
Organ Transplantation
  • Transfers organs (kidney, liver, heart); ~150,000 global transplants in 2024 (WHO).
  • Perfusion systems extend organ viability to 24+ hours; DCD organs up 20% since 2010.
  • Xenotransplantation: 2024 pig kidney transplant; gene-edited organs reduce rejection by 80%.
3D Bioprinting for Transplants
  • Prints tissues using bioinks; aims for functional organs (kidney, liver).
  • 2025: Mayo Clinic’s vascularized liver tissue; skin grafts for burns achieve 95% success.
  • Advancements: Microfluidic vascularization; preclinical kidney scaffolds viable 6 months.
Immunosuppressive Therapy
  • Prevents transplant rejection via drugs (tacrolimus, cyclosporine); used in 90% of transplants.
  • Biologics (belatacept) reduce side effects; 2025 RNAi trials silence immune genes.
  • Outcomes: 1-year kidney graft survival ~95%; long-term rejection down 30% since 2000.
Organoids
  • Miniaturized organ models from stem cells; used for drug testing, disease modeling.
  • 2025: Brain, liver organoids model Alzheimer’s, hepatitis; 90% accuracy in drug response prediction.
  • Applications: Personalized medicine; preclinical transplant tissue testing; vascularized organoids emerging.
Precision Medicine and Surgery
CyberKnife Radiosurgery
  • Robotic stereotactic radiotherapy; sub-millimeter accuracy for tumor ablation.
  • Treats brain, prostate cancers in 1-5 sessions; 95% control for vestibular schwannoma (2025).
  • Advancements: Synchrony tracks moving tumors; donut-shaped SBRT protects spinal cord.
LASIK Eye Surgery
  • Laser corneal reshaping for refractive errors; femtosecond/excimer lasers.
  • Contoura Vision (2025): Topography-guided, 20/20+ vision in 90%+ cases.
  • Advancements: SMILE (flapless, 2016); AI eye-tracking; treats myopia up to -12D.
da Vinci Surgical System
  • Robotic surgery platform for minimally invasive procedures; used in urology, gynecology.
  • Over 10M procedures by 2025; reduces recovery time by 50% vs. open surgery.
  • Advancements: Haptic feedback (2024); AI-assisted navigation improves precision by 20%.
Pharmacogenomics
  • Uses DNA profiling to tailor drug treatments; optimizes efficacy, reduces adverse effects.
  • 2025: Guides 30% of oncology prescriptions; CYP2D6 testing for antidepressants improves response by 25%.
  • Applications: Cancer, cardiology; integrated with DNA fingerprinting for rapid diagnostics.
- Important communication technologies (unverified) -
Technology Key Features/Advancements
Mobile Network Generations
1G (First Generation)
  • AMPS/TACS standard in U.S./UK (introduced 1979); analog cellular networks, voice-only, ~2.4 Kbps.
  • First commercial deployment: Japan (NTT, 1979); enabled mobile phones, ~10 km cell radius.
  • Limitations: Poor voice quality, no encryption, high power consumption; phased out by 2000s.
2G (Second Generation)
  • GSM/CDMA standards (1991); digital cellular networks, 9.6-14.4 Kbps, introduced SMS.
  • Global adoption: 90% coverage by 2000; enabled roaming, secure calls via encryption.
  • 2.5G (GPRS), 56-114 Kbps added basic data (email, WAP); 2.75G (EDGE), ~384 Kbps.
3G (Third Generation)
  • UMTS/CDMA2000 (2001); digital mobile broadband, 384 Kbps-2 Mbps, enabled video calls, mobile internet.
  • 3.5G (HSDPA), 7.2-14 Mbps improved data rates; global 3G users ~2B by 2010.
  • Applications: Mobile apps, streaming; standardized by ITU’s IMT-2000; phased out in many regions by 2025.
4G/LTE (Fourth Generation/Long-Term Evolution)
  • LTE standard (2009); all-IP mobile broadband, 10-100 Mbps, low latency (~20 ms).
  • LTE-Advanced (2013): Up to 1 Gbps, carrier aggregation; supports HD video, VoLTE.
  • Global coverage: ~80% by 2020; 2025 LTE-A Pro (3 Gbps) enhances IoT, smart cities.
5G (Fifth Generation)
  • NR standard (2019); high-speed, low-latency networks, 1-10 Gbps, latency <5 ms.
  • Features: mmWave for ultra-fast speeds, massive MIMO, network slicing for IoT, autonomous vehicles.
  • 2025: 2.5B global users; standalone 5G cores enable edge computing, smart factories; India’s 5G covers 90% urban areas.
6G (Sixth Generation, Emerging)
  • Next-gen mobile network (research phase, 2025); targets 100 Gbps-1 Tbps, latency <1 ms.
  • Features: Terahertz bands, AI-driven networks, holographic communication, global satellite integration.
  • 2025: Early trials in China, Japan; India’s 6G Vision aims for 2030 deployment.
Wireless LAN Technologies
WiFi (IEEE 802.11 Standards)
  • 802.11b (1997, 11 Mbps, 2.4 GHz) popularized WiFi; evolved to 802.11n/802.11ac/802.11ax.
  • WiFi 6 (802.11ax) (2019): 9.6 Gbps, MU-MIMO, OFDMA; reduces congestion, supports IoT.
  • WiFi 7 (802.11be) (2024): 46 Gbps, 320 MHz channels; 2025 adoption in 20% of devices, enhances AR/VR.
WiFi HaLow (802.11ah)
  • 802.11ah (2016); low-power, long-range WiFi, 900 MHz band, 1-15 Mbps, up to 1 km range.
  • Applications: IoT, smart homes, agriculture; supports 8,000+ devices per access point.
  • 2025: Deployed in 10% of IoT networks; low energy use extends battery life by 5x vs. standard WiFi.
Short-Range and Other Wireless Technologies
Bluetooth
  • Bluetooth 5.0 (1998, 2016 update); short-range wireless, 2.4 GHz, 1-3 Mbps, range up to 240 m.
  • Applications: Audio streaming, wearables, IoT; LE Audio (2022) enhances hearing aids, multi-stream.
  • 2025: Bluetooth 5.4 supports Auracast (broadcast audio); 2B devices shipped annually.
Zigbee
  • Zigbee 3.0 (2004); low-power mesh network, 2.4 GHz, 250 Kbps, range ~100 m; IEEE 802.15.4.
  • Applications: Smart homes, industrial IoT; supports 65,000+ nodes per network.
  • 2025: Used in 500M+ devices, enhances home automation security.
NFC (Near Field Communication)
  • NFC (2004); short-range (4-10 cm) wireless, 13.56 MHz, 424 Kbps; contactless payments, tags.
  • Applications: Mobile payments (50% of global transactions by 2025), access control.
  • 2025: NFC Forum’s multi-purpose tap supports ticketing, loyalty programs; 5B+ enabled devices.
Ultra-Wideband (UWB)
  • UWB (2002); high-precision short-range wireless, 3.1-10.6 GHz, 480 Mbps, <10 cm accuracy.
  • Applications: Indoor positioning, asset tracking, smart keys; used in Apple AirTag, car access.
  • 2025: UWB 2.0 doubles range to 200 m; adopted in 30% of smartphones for secure ranging.
Emerging and Specialized Technologies
Li-Fi (Light Fidelity)
  • Li-Fi (2011); uses visible light for data transfer, 100 Mbps-224 Gbps, LED-based, secure indoor networks.
  • Applications: High-speed internet in RF-sensitive areas (hospitals, aircraft); no spectrum interference.
  • 2025: IEEE 802.11bb standard; pilot deployments in 500+ offices, 10x faster than WiFi 6 in tests.
Satellite Communication (LEO Constellations)
  • Starlink, OneWeb (1990s, 2020s); Low Earth Orbit satellites, 50-200 Mbps globally.
  • Features: Low latency (~20 ms), broadband for remote areas; Starlink has 5,000+ satellites by 2025.
  • Applications: Rural internet, disaster recovery; 2025 India deployments cover 80% remote areas.
mmWave Communication
  • mmWave (24-100 GHz) for 5G/6G; 1-10 Gbps, supports ultra-dense networks.
  • Applications: Urban 5G, stadiums, VR streaming; short range (~200 m) due to signal attenuation.
  • 2025: 30% of 5G base stations use mmWave; 6G trials achieve 100 Gbps in lab settings.
Software-Defined Radio (SDR)
  • SDR (1990s); programmable radio systems, supports multiple protocols (LTE, WiFi) on single hardware.
  • Applications: Military comms, disaster response; enables rapid protocol switching.
  • 2025: SDR in 20% of 5G base stations; AI-driven modulation improves spectrum efficiency by 30%.
- Important GM crops with details (unverified) -
Crop/Technology Key Details
Major GM Crops by Acreage (Global, 2023-2025 Data)
Soybean
  • Traits: Herbicide-tolerant (Roundup Ready, glyphosate resistance via Agrobacterium gene); Bt insect-resistant varieties emerging.
  • Acreage: ~130M ha (78% of global soybean); top producers: USA (50%), Brazil (30%), Argentina (15%).
  • Impact: Boosts yields 20-30%, cuts herbicide use 37%; used in oils, animal feed (70% of processed foods).
  • India: No commercial GM soybean; imports GM soy for feed, trials ongoing for local cultivation.
Maize (Corn)
  • Traits: Bt (resistant to corn borer/rootworm), herbicide-tolerant (Roundup Ready); stacked traits dominate.
  • Acreage: ~60M ha (30% of global maize); top producers: USA (40%), Brazil (25%), Argentina (15%).
  • Impact: Reduces mycotoxin by 50%, yields up 10-20%; key for ethanol, feed, corn syrup.
  • India: Bt maize trials ongoing; no commercial approval, imports GM maize for feed.
Cotton
  • Traits: Bt (resistant to bollworm/boll weevil); herbicide-tolerant varieties (glyphosate).
  • Acreage: ~25M ha (76% of global cotton); top producers: India (11.6M ha), USA (6M ha), China (4M ha).
  • Impact: Cuts pesticide use 37%, yields up 25%; ~$150B economic gain globally since 1996.
  • India: Bt cotton (2002) covers 95% of cotton area; production tripled (13M to 37M bales, 2002-2023).
Canola (Oilseed Rape)
  • Traits: Herbicide-tolerant (glyphosate/glufosinate); low erucic acid for edible oil.
  • Acreage: ~10M ha (25% of global); top producers: Canada (8M ha), USA (1M ha), Australia (0.5M ha).
  • Impact: Yields up 20%, supports biodiesel, cooking oil; reduces tillage for soil conservation.
  • India: No GM canola; GM mustard (Dhara Mustard Hybrid-11) trials ongoing, no approval.
Specialty GM Crops
Papaya
  • Traits: Virus-resistant (Rainbow papaya with PRSV coat protein gene).
  • Acreage: ~0.5M ha; top producers: USA (Hawaii, 80%), China.
  • Impact: Saved Hawaii’s industry from PRSV; yields up 4x, supports global exports.
  • India: No GM papaya; virus-resistant trials stalled due to biosafety concerns.
Eggplant (Brinjal)
  • Traits: Bt (resistant to fruit/shoot borer).
  • Acreage: ~0.01M ha; top producer: Bangladesh (12 ha since 2014).
  • Impact: Cuts pesticide use 50%, yields up 30%; benefits small farmers in Asia.
  • India: Bt brinjal approved 2009, moratorium since 2010; regulatory debates ongoing.
Alfalfa
  • Traits: Herbicide-tolerant (Roundup Ready); low lignin for better digestibility.
  • Acreage: ~1M ha; top producer: USA (90%).
  • Impact: Enhances livestock feed, cuts weed control costs 50%; supports dairy/meat.
  • India: No GM alfalfa; limited research due to low domestic demand.
Sugar Beet
  • Traits: Herbicide-tolerant (Roundup Ready); virus-resistant.
  • Acreage: ~0.5M ha (50% of global); top producers: USA (95%), Canada.
  • Impact: Sugar yield up 20%, reduced chemical inputs; 20% of world sugar supply.
  • India: No GM sugar beet; Bt sugarcane research ongoing.
Apple
  • Traits: Non-browning (Arctic apples, silenced polyphenol oxidase gene).
  • Acreage: ~0.01M ha; top producer: USA.
  • Impact: Reduces food waste (30% less browning); approved 2015, enhances fresh-cut market.
  • India: No GM apple; focus on conventional horticulture.
Potato
  • Traits: Late blight resistance (via wild potato genes); non-browning (Innate potato).
  • Acreage: ~0.1M ha; top producer: USA.
  • Impact: Cuts fungicide use 40%, reduces bruising losses; used in processed foods (chips).
  • India: GM potato trials (Amflora) suspended; focus on disease-resistant breeding.
Nutritionally Enhanced GM Crops
Golden Rice
  • Traits: Biofortified with beta-carotene (vitamin A) via daffodil/maize genes.
  • Acreage: Pilot trials; approved in Philippines (2021), Bangladesh (2022).
  • Impact: Addresses vitamin A deficiency (190M children); provides 50% daily requirement.
  • India: Field trials approved 2018; regulatory delays, potential for rice-heavy diets.
Biofortified Banana
  • Traits: Enhanced vitamin A via Queensland University genes; targets malnutrition.
  • Acreage: Pilot trials in Australia, Uganda; not yet commercial by 2025.
  • Impact: Provides 60% daily vitamin A; aimed at East Africa, where bananas are staple.
  • India: Research interest; no trials, focus on local banana varieties.
Experimental/Pipeline GM Crops
Mustard
  • Traits: Dhara Mustard Hybrid-11 (DMH-11); barnase-barstar for hybrid vigor, herbicide-tolerant.
  • Acreage: Field trials in India; no commercial release by 2025.
  • Impact: Potential 25-30% yield increase; reduces edible oil imports (India imports 60% of oil).
  • India: Approved 2022 by GEAC, but Supreme Court stay; biosafety debates ongoing.
Wheat
  • Traits: Drought-tolerant (HB4, Argentina); herbicide-tolerant (Roundup Ready).
  • Acreage: ~0.05M ha; approved in Argentina (2020), Brazil (2021); limited adoption.
  • Impact: Improves yields in water-scarce areas by 20%; supports global wheat supply.
  • India: No GM wheat; research on drought tolerance, no field trials by 2025.
Sugarcane
  • Traits: Bt (insect-resistant to stem borer); drought-tolerant varieties in development.
  • Acreage: Field trials in Brazil, India; no commercial release by 2025.
  • Impact: Potential 15-20% yield increase; reduces pesticide use in tropical regions.
  • India: Bt sugarcane trials ongoing; regulatory approval pending biosafety data.
Drought-Tolerant Maize
  • Traits: DroughtGard (cspB gene); enhances water stress tolerance.
  • Acreage: ~0.5M ha; top producer: USA, trials in Africa.
  • Impact: Yields stable under 30% less water; critical for Sub-Saharan Africa.
  • India: Trials in drought-prone states; no commercial approval by 2025.

Category Key Systems
Missiles (Ballistic, Cruise, SAM, AAM, ATGM)
Ballistic Missiles (Surface-to-Surface)
  • Prithvi-II: Short-range ballistic missile (SRBM), range 350 km, liquid-fueled, nuclear-capable; inducted 2003, used for tactical strikes.
  • Agni-V: Intercontinental ballistic missile (ICBM), range 5,000-5,500 km, MIRV-capable, canister-launched; inducted 2021, enhances nuclear deterrence.
  • Agni-P: Medium-range ballistic missile (MRBM), range 1,000-2,000 km, solid-fueled, maneuverable re-entry vehicle; tested 2023, operational 2025.
  • K-4: Submarine-launched ballistic missile (SLBM), range 3,500 km, nuclear-capable; deployed on Arihant-class SSBNs, tested 2024.
Cruise Missiles
  • BrahMos: Supersonic cruise missile, range 290-800 km (extended variant), multi-platform (land/sea/air); inducted 2005, hypersonic version tested 2025.
  • Nirbhay: Subsonic cruise missile, range 1,000 km, low-altitude stealthy flight; inducted 2020, used for precision strikes.
Surface-to-Air Missiles (SAM) & Air Defense
  • Akash-NG: Medium-range SAM, range 70 km, indigenous, intercepts aircraft/drones; inducted 2023, 2 regiments operational.
  • S-400 Triumf: Long-range SAM, range 400 km, multi-target engagement; 5 squadrons inducted 2021-2025, key in Operation Sindoor.
  • Barak-8 (MR-SAM/LR-SAM): Medium/long-range SAM, range 70-100 km, Indo-Israeli; deployed on ships/jets, 18 systems by 2025.
  • Project Kusha (XRSAM): Long-range SAM, range 350 km, under development; trials 2025, to rival S-400.
Air-to-Air Missiles (AAM) & Anti-Tank Guided Missiles (ATGM)
  • Astra Mk-1: Beyond-visual-range AAM, range 110 km, indigenous; integrated on Su-30MKI, 200+ inducted 2025.
  • Meteor: Long-range AAM, range 150+ km, ramjet-powered; on Rafale jets, 250 procured 2022-2025.
  • Nag: Third-gen ATGM, range 4-7 km, fire-and-forget; inducted 2019, used in NAMICA carriers.
  • Helina (Dhruvastra): Helicopter-launched ATGM, range 7-10 km, anti-tank; operational on ALH Dhruv, 120+ by 2025.
Naval Assets (Ships & Submarines)
Aircraft Carriers & Amphibious Ships
  • INS Vikrant (1961, legacy): Majestic-class aircraft carrier, displacement 19,500 tons, commissioned 1961, decommissioned 1997; pivotal in 1971 Indo-Pak War, now a museum ship (scrap status debated).
  • INS Vikrant (2013): Indigenous aircraft carrier, displacement 45,000 tons, 30+ aircraft capacity; commissioned 2022, fully operational November 2023 with MiG-29K/Rafale M.
  • INS Viraat (legacy): Centaur-class carrier, displacement 28,500 tons, commissioned 1987, decommissioned 2017; served in 1999 Kargil ops, now a museum.
  • INS Vikramaditya: Modified Kiev-class carrier, displacement 45,400 tons, 36 aircraft; inducted 2013, key for blue-water ops.
  • INS Jalashwa: Amphibious transport dock, displacement 16,800 tons, 1,000 troops/vehicles; inducted 2007, supports landing ops.
Destroyers & Frigates
  • INS Surat (Project 15B): Stealth guided-missile destroyer, displacement 7,500 tons, BrahMos/S-400 integration; commissioned Jan 2025.
  • INS Kolkata (Project 15A): Stealth destroyer, displacement 7,400 tons, anti-ship/air defense; commissioned 2014.
  • INS Nilgiri (Project 17A): Stealth multi-role frigate, displacement 6,670 tons, advanced sensors; commissioned Jan 2025.
  • INS Teg (Talwar-class): Frigate, displacement 4,000 tons, stealth features; Russian-built, inducted 2012.
Submarines
  • INS Arihant: Nuclear-powered ballistic missile submarine (SSBN), displacement 6,000 tons, K-15 Sagarika missiles; commissioned 2016.
  • INS Arighat: Arihant-class SSBN, displacement 7,000 tons, K-4 missiles; commissioned 2024.
  • INS Vagsheer (Kalvari-class): Scorpene diesel-electric submarine, displacement 1,775 tons, stealthy AIP; commissioned Jan 2025, last of 6.
  • INS Chakra III: Leased Akula-class nuclear attack submarine (SSN), displacement 8,140 tons, torpedo/missile capable; leased 2025 from Russia.
  • INS Sindhughosh (Kilo-class, legacy): Diesel-electric submarine, displacement 3,076 tons; 8 operational, upgraded for extended service till 2030.
Air Force & Army Aviation Assets (Jets, Aircraft, Helicopters)
Fighter Jets & Multirole Aircraft
  • Su-30MKI: Air superiority fighter, range 3,000 km, thrust-vectoring; 272 inducted, backbone of IAF fleet.
  • Rafale: Multirole fighter, range 3,700 km, Meteor/Astra missiles; 36 inducted 2020, 26 more for Navy by 2030.
  • Tejas Mk-1A: Indigenous light combat aircraft, range 1,850 km, multi-role; 83 ordered 2021, first deliveries 2025; total 180 planned to replace MiG-21.
  • MiG-29UPG: Upgraded multirole fighter, range 2,100 km, air superiority; 65 operational, upgraded 2011-2025, to be replaced by Tejas Mk2.
  • MiG-21 Bison: Supersonic interceptor, range 1,300 km, legacy fighter; retired September 2025 after 62 years; key in 1971/Kargil wars, 50+ crashes.
  • MiG-27 (legacy): Ground-attack fighter, range 1,600 km; retired 2019, served in IPKF/1999 ops.
  • MiG-23 (legacy): Interceptor/strike fighter, range 1,800 km; retired 2009, used in 1971/Siachen.
  • Mirage 2000: Multirole strike fighter, range 1,550 km, nuclear-capable; 45 operational, upgraded 2024.
  • Jaguar: Ground-attack fighter, range 1,900 km, deep strike; 120 operational, to be replaced by Tejas Mk2/AMCA.
Transport & Reconnaissance Aircraft
  • C-17 Globemaster III: Strategic airlifter, payload 77 tons, long-range; 11 inducted, supports rapid deployment in LAC operations.
  • C-295: Tactical transport, payload 9 tons, indigenous production; 56 ordered 2023, first inducted 2025.
  • P-8I Poseidon: Maritime patrol aircraft, range 7,500 km, anti-submarine; 12 inducted, 6 more by 2025 for Andaman ops.
  • An-32 (legacy): Tactical transport, payload 6 tons; 100+ operational, upgrades ongoing till 2032.
Helicopters
  • AH-64E Apache: Attack helicopter, payload 16 Hellfire missiles, 30mm chain gun; 22 inducted 2019-2022 for IAF, 6 for Army Aviation, key for anti-armor ops along LAC.
  • CH-47F Chinook: Heavy-lift helicopter, payload 12 tons, range 740 km; 15 inducted 2019-2021, supports high-altitude ops in Ladakh/Siachen.
  • ALH Dhruv: Indigenous utility helicopter, payload 2.5 tons, range 640 km; 145+ inducted (IAF/Army), Helina missile integration 2025, used in disaster relief.
  • LCH Prachand: Indigenous light combat helicopter, payload 1 ton (missiles/guns), altitude 6,500 m; 10 inducted 2022-2025, designed for high-altitude combat, 156 more ordered.
  • Chetak/Cheetah (legacy): Utility helicopter, payload 1 ton, range 500 km; 200+ operational (HAL/Alouette), used in Siachen, upgrades till 2030.
Army Assets (Tanks & Artillery)
Main Battle Tanks (MBT)
  • T-90 Bhishma: MBT, weight 48 tons, 125mm gun; 2,000+ inducted, produced locally, upgraded 2025 with Invar missiles.
  • Arjun Mk-1A: Indigenous MBT, weight 68 tons, Kanchan armor; 118 inducted 2021, enhanced fire control.
  • Zorawar: Light tank, weight 25 tons, high-altitude ops; prototypes tested 2025, for LAC deployment.
  • T-72 Ajeya: Upgraded MBT, weight 41 tons, 125mm gun; 2,400+ operational, ongoing upgrades for future readiness.
  • T-55/Vijayanta (legacy): Medium tank, weight 36 tons, 105mm gun; retired 2010s, served in 1965/1971 wars, ~1,600 once in service.
Artillery Systems
  • Dhanush: 155mm towed howitzer, range 38 km, indigenous; 114 inducted 2022-2025, field artillery rationalization.
  • K9 Vajra-T: Self-propelled howitzer, range 40 km, tracked; 100 inducted 2018, 100 more by 2025.
  • Pinaka Mk-II: Multi-barrel rocket launcher (MBRL), range 60 km, guided rockets; 10 regiments operational 2025.
  • ATAGS: Advanced towed artillery gun, range 48 km, 155mm/52 cal; 307 ordered March 2025, trials completed.
  • M-46 (legacy): 130mm towed field gun, range 27 km; ~500 operational, upgrades to 155mm via barrel replacement.
Integrated Defense Systems & Other Assets
Ballistic Missile Defense (BMD) & Counter-Drone
  • Indian BMD Programme (Phase-II): Two-tier shield, exo/endo-atmospheric interceptors; tested July 2024, Delhi/Mumbai coverage by 2026.
  • Sudarshan Chakra: Integrated air/missile defense network, multi-layered; announced Aug 2025, includes Kusha SAM.
  • SAMAR: Quick-reaction SAM, range 20-30 km, repurposed R-73 missiles; inducted 2024, used in Operation Sindoor.
Infantry Weapons & UAVs
  • INSAS 1B1: Assault rifle, 5.56mm, upgraded; 500,000+ in service, phasing to SIG 716.
  • Pinaka Loitering Munition: Kamikaze drone, range 15 km, precision strike; inducted 2024, 100+ units.
  • Heron TP: MALE UAV, endurance 36 hrs, surveillance; 25+ operational, Israeli-origin.

Ballistic Missiles (ICBM/SLBM/MRBM)

  • Trident II D5 (USA/UK): Submarine-launched ballistic missile (SLBM), range 12,000 km, MIRV-capable; deployed on Ohio/Vanguard-class subs, 14 missiles per sub.
  • RS-28 Sarmat (Russia): ICBM, range 18,000 km, MIRV (10-15 warheads), heaviest at 208 tons; operational 2023, silo-based for global reach.

  • Agni-V (India): ICBM, range 5,000-8,000 km, MIRV-capable; road-mobile, inducted 2021 for Asia-Europe coverage.
  • M51 (France): SLBM, range 10,000 km, MIRV; deployed on Triomphant-class subs, core of French nuclear deterrent.
Cruise Missiles
  • Tomahawk (USA): Subsonic land-attack cruise missile (LACM), range 1,600 km, precision-guided; Block V upgrades 2025, used in 2,000+ strikes since 1991.
  • Kh-101 (Russia): Stealthy air-launched LACM, range 2,500-5,000 km, conventional/nuclear; deployed on Tu-95/Tu-160 bombers, used in Ukraine 2022-2025.
  • BrahMos (India/Russia): Supersonic cruise missile, range 290-800 km (extended), multi-platform; hypersonic variant tested 2025, exported to Philippines.
  • Storm Shadow/SCALP (UK/France): Air-launched LACM, range 560 km, bunker-buster; used in Libya 2011, Ukraine 2023-2025.
  • YJ-18 (China): Anti-ship/supersonic LACM, range 540 km, Mach 3 terminal speed; deployed on Type 052D destroyers, Type 093 subs.
Surface-to-Air Missiles (SAM) & Air Defense
  • S-400 Triumf (Russia): Long-range SAM, range 400 km, multi-target (36+); exported to China/India/Turkey, 5 regiments in Russia, intercepts ballistic missiles.
  • Patriot PAC-3 (USA): Medium/long-range SAM, range 160 km, hit-to-kill interceptor; 1,000+ systems globally, used in Saudi Arabia vs. Houthi drones 2019-2025.
  • Iron Dome (Israel): Short-range SAM, range 70 km, anti-rocket/drone; 90% intercept rate, 10+ batteries, key in Gaza conflicts 2021-2025.
  • THAAD (USA): Terminal high-altitude defense, range 200 km, exo-atmospheric intercept; 7 batteries deployed (South Korea, Guam), vs. North Korean missiles.
  • HQ-9 (China): Long-range SAM, range 200 km, similar to S-300; 200+ launchers, integrated with J-20 fighters for layered defense.
Air-to-Air Missiles (AAM) & Anti-Tank Guided Missiles (ATGM)
  • AIM-120 AMRAAM (USA): Beyond-visual-range AAM, range 160 km, active radar; 20,000+ produced, integrated on F-35/F-22, NASAMS ground systems.
  • R-77 (Russia): Medium-range AAM, range 110 km, thrust-vectoring; deployed on Su-35/Su-57, used in Syria/Ukraine.
  • Meteor (Europe): Ramjet-powered AAM, range 200 km, no-escape zone; on Rafale/Eurofighter, exported to India/Qatar.
  • J-10C PL-15 (China): Long-range AAM, range 200 km, active seeker; integrated on J-20, counters F-35 in South China Sea.
  • Spike (Israel): Multi-platform ATGM, range 30 km, fire-and-forget; 30,000+ systems, used by 30+ countries for anti-tank roles.

Air Force & Army Aviation Assets (Fighter Jets, Helicopters, Transport Aircraft)
Fighter Jets

- CHAPTER 1: BIOTECHNOLOGY - - 1. WHAT IS DNA? - DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) can be looked at in two ways: - As code of life - As vehicle of heredity - DNA AS CODE OF LIFE - DNA carries the genetic information in the form of genes, which encode a complete set of instructions for building and maintaining an organism. - The code is present in the nucleus of every cell of every living organism (with few exceptions like RBC, certain viruses etc.) - The code is read by a host of machineries in the cell to build proteins which are the building blocks of life. (Proteins constitute 55% of the dry mass of the cell. Everything from the cell's structure to enzymes are made of proteins.) - BUILDING BLOCKS OF DNA - The building blocks of DNA are called nucleotides. Each nucleotide is made up of three main components: - (i) Sugar molecule: - A five-carbon sugar molecule, called deoxyribose, provides structural support to the DNA molecule. - (ii) Phosphate group: - Phosphate group attached to the deoxyribose sugar molecule. The phosphate groups contribute to the overall negative charge of the DNA molecule and play a role in linking nucleotides together to form the sugar-phosphate backbone of the DNA strand. - (iii) Nitrogenous base (4 bases): - Adenine (A), Guanine (G), Cytosine (C) and Thymine (T). - The specific sequence of these four nitrogenous bases determines the genetic code. These bases pair up specifically (A-T and C-G) forming the "rungs" of the double helical structure of DNA. (So if you know one strand you know what is on the other) - Note: - While in nature only 4 lettered-DNA is found, scientists have recently created a synthetic DNA with 8 letters. - The human genome is made of 3 billion base pairs. (So total 6 billion bases) - 2. WHAT IS CHROMOSOME? - Chromosomes are thread-like structures found in the nucleus of cells that carry genetic information. They are composed of DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) and proteins. - Normally, each cell in the human body has 23 pairs of chromosomes (46 total chromosomes). This includes 22 pairs of autosomes plus one pair of sex chromosomes. Females have two X chromosomes (XX), while males have one X and one Y chromosome (XY). - The 23 pairs of chromosomes together have 3.2 billion base-pairs of DNA. - 3. WHAT IS GENOME? - A genome is an organism’s complete set of DNA or genetic material. It is a collection of all the genes and the regions between the genes contained in our 23 pairs of chromosomes. - 4. WHAT IS MITOCHONDRIAL DNA? - In addition to the nucleus, some DNA is also present in the mitochondria (mtDNA). - Mitochondria are cellular organelles found in the cytoplasm. They are called the powerhouse of the cell as they generate most of the cell's energy currency- ATP (adenosine triphosphate). - mtDNA codes for a small number of genes, essential for the proper functioning of mitochondria. In humans, mtDNA is circular and much smaller (consists of about 16,500 base pairs), compared to nuclear DNA. - Inheritance: - Unlike nuclear DNA which come from both parents, mtDNA is inherited almost exclusively from the mother. This is because mitochondria are primarily contributed by the egg cell during fertilisation. While sperm cells do contain mitochondria, these mitochondria are typically not incorporated into the developing embryo. - 5. WHAT IS RNA? - - RNA (Ribonucleic acid) is a single-stranded molecule/nucleic acid found in most living organisms and viruses. It is made up of nucleotides, which are ribose sugars attached to nitrogenous bases and phosphate groups. - The nitrogenous bases include adenine (A), guanine (G), uracil (U) and cytosine (C). - Major types of RNA: - mRNA: - messenger RNA molecules copy the genetic information/instruction from DNA and carry it to ribosomes, where protein synthesis occurs. In order to transcribe, mRNA uses an enzyme called RNA polymerase. - tRNA: - transfer RNA assists in translation of mRNA into proteins by bringing amino acids to the ribosome. tRNA brings amino acids that are lying in the cell after reading 3 letters of mRNA at a time (3 letters or codon translate into one amino acid). - rRNA: - ribosomal RNA molecules are structural components of ribosomes (cellular structures where protein synthesis takes place). - RNA is generally less stable than DNA due to the presence of an extra hydroxyl (OH) group on the ribose sugar in RNA. This makes RNA more prone to degradation by enzymes called ribonucleases. DNA, with its deoxyribose sugar lacking the extra OH group, is more stable and less susceptible to enzymatic degradation. - 6. CENTRAL DOGMA - - Central dogma of molecular biology explains how genetic information flows from DNA to RNA to proteins. - Gene expression is the process our cells use to convert instructions in DNA into proteins. This happens in two stages called transcription (occurs in the nucleus, in eukaryotic organisms) and translation (occurs in cytoplasm). - - Transcription: - mRNA copies the sequence of a gene from DNA, and then leaves the nucleus and travels to cytoplasm. - Translation: - Using the template encoded by mRNA, translation is carried out by ribosomes, i.e., the information in the mRNA is used to synthesise proteins (building blocks of life). - CENTRAL DOGMA (COOKBOOK ANALOGY) - - Consider DNA to be a cookbook of recipes for making proteins. Transcription is the process of photocopying the recipe (done by mRNA inside the nucleus). - Once copied, the recipe mRNA goes outside the nucleus to the chef (ribosome). There is also a translator, tRNA, who translates mRNA recipe to rRNA of the ribosome which then makes amino acids (the building blocks of proteins). - A block of 3 letters in the mRNA corresponds to ‘cooking’ of 1 amino acid. Each 3-letter base in mRNA that is read by tRNA that corresponds to one amino acid is called ‘codon’. - Many amino acids come together to form the primary structure of protein. The primary structure is twisted and folded to make a 3-D structure of protein. - 7. GENES & EPIGENETICS - The Human Genome Project (completed in 2003) found that only 1.5% of the total human genome (3 billion base pairs) codes for proteins. (The coding parts of protein are called genes) - It also found that humans have about 20,000–25,000 genes. The remaining 98.5% of the genome is non-coding, meaning it does not provide instructions for making proteins. - EPIGENETICS- IT’S ALL ABOUT GENE EXPRESSION - For a long time, non-coding regions of the genome (98.5%) were thought to be ‘dark area’ or ‘Junk DNA’, because they did not appear to have any specific purpose. - However, new studies suggest that they play a huge role in gene expression, which has become an important area of study in recent times called epigenetics. - Epigenetics deals with the processes that control how the genes are expressed. - We know that all the cells in our body have the same genome. The average human body contains approximately 37.2 trillion cells and about 220 different cell types. - If the same code (genome) exists in the nucleus of all these cells, how do 220 odd cell types make up for 4 different tissue types and 78 different organ types, all working in unison to make human life possible? - The answer lies in gene expression. Different genes are expressed in different cells that perform different functions that look differently (like your heart cell and your kidney cell). - INTRONS, EXONS AND RNA SPLICING - - As we have seen, mRNA is a copy of only the coding part of DNA (gene). - The coding part does not occur on chromosomes in one single sequence as one whole. It is spread out on a chromosome in parts. Each part is separated by a non-coding part of the genome. - In fact, 25% of all the non-coding parts occur in between genes. - The non-coding parts between genes are called introns and the coding parts that mRNA is interested in are called exons. - So, mRNA must copy only exons and cut out all the introns. This cutting of introns to join only exons is called RNA splicing. - The final mRNA after splicing of introns is called exome (which represents only 1.5% of the genome). It is this final mRNA after splicing that is important for coding for protein. - MUSCULAR DYSTROPHY - One type of muscular dystrophy, a genetic disease, is a result of a defect in RNA splicing while copying the X-chromosome. - Since it is associated with the X-chromosome, it is more prevalent in males as they have only one X-chromosome. - 8. GENOME SEQUENCING: READING THE CODE OF LIFE - Genome sequencing in simple words is reading the entire book of genome letter by letter (base by base). It is a procedure for determining the precise order of the four nucleotide bases (A, T, C, G) in DNA. - SEQUENCING TECHNOLOGIES: - Short-read: - Earlier approach to sequencing, where the genome was chopped into small fragments which could be reassembled like a jigsaw puzzle. In this approach, we read 150 bases at a time. Reading short segments of the genome was time-consuming and labor-intensive. - Next-Generation sequencing: - Use of computers to read multiple fragments at the same time in an automated process which makes whole-genome sequencing relatively faster, accurate, automated, and cheap (dominant method). - Nanopore sequencing: - Use of nanotechnology enabled reading longer sequences of the genome (2.3 million base-long sequences at once). The technique is also suitable for identifying pathogens in food, disease control, and diagnosis of infection. - APPLICATIONS OF GENOME SEQUENCING - Disease diagnosis and Personalized drug development: - Identify genetic mutations and variations, evaluate rare disorders, and even cancer from a genetic viewpoint. E.g., Mutation in MYBPC3 (Cardiac Myosin binding protein) leads to cardiac arrest at a young age (found in 4.5% of the Indian population but is rare globally). - Agriculture: - Help identify genes that contribute to desirable traits in plants and animals, allowing for selective breeding of crops and livestock. - Tool for prenatal screening to investigate whether a fetus has genetic disorders/anomalies. - Identify suspects in criminal investigations and establish paternity in cases of disputed parentage. - Help trace the evolutionary history of species and understand the mechanisms underlying evolution. - IMPORTANT GENOME SEQUENCING INITIATIVES - Human Genome Project (1990-2003): - Led to the decoding of the entire human genome for the first time, giving the ability to read nature’s complete genetic blueprint. - IndiGen (completed 2019): - Initiated by the Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR) from April 2019 to October 2019. Aim: To sequence whole genomes of 1029 individuals from diverse ethnic groups across India. - Genome India Project (2020-2023): - To create a comprehensive reference database of genetic variations and conduct data analysis of 10,000 individuals representing India’s diverse population. The dataset is stored at the Indian Biological Data Centre and made available as a digital public good for research. - Genomics in Indian Agriculture: - Includes Wheat Genome Sequencing Programme, Rice Functional Genomics, National Plant Gene Repository programme, Next Generation Challenge Programme on Chickpea Genomics, and INDIGAU (Genomics of cow). - National Genomic Grid: - Aims to collect cancer cells and tissues to facilitate cancer research in India. Uses genome sequencing to study genomic factors influencing cancer in the Indian population. - Earth Bio-genome Project: - An international effort to sequence and digitize the genomes of every eukaryotic species on Earth over 10 years. It is an open-source DNA database useful for planning environmental conservation initiatives. - Human Microbiome Project: - Studies genes of microbes in the human body, including gut, skin, oral cavity, and vagina, to understand their role in human health and diseases. The human body contains roughly 10 times more microbial cells than human cells. - DeepVariant: - AI in genomics: Google’s AI system that converts sequencing data from high-throughput sequencing into an accurate picture of the entire genome. - AlphaFold (Nobel Prize in Chemistry 2024): - Google’s AI system capable of predicting protein structures, important for understanding diseases and drug development. - Data-Driven Research to Eradicate TB Initiative (2022): - Genome sequencing initiative to improve understanding of drug-resistant TB and capture unique genomic features of Mycobacterium tuberculosis in India. Target: sequencing 32,500 samples (10,000 sequenced till March 2025). Genomic dataset potential: improves diagnostic accuracy, enables faster resistance profiling, develops advanced tests reducing TB confirmation time from weeks to hours/days, and allows tailoring treatment to individual patients. - 9. CELL-FREE DNA - Cell-free DNA (cfDNA) refers to fragments of DNA found in bodily fluids such as blood, urine, saliva, and cerebrospinal fluid. - They are released into the bloodstream by cells that underwent programmed cell death or an unplanned cell death due to any injury or disease. - These degraded (non-functional) fragments of nucleic acids do not possess the ability to replicate or carry out cellular processes. - However, cfDNA can carry genetic information from the cells they originated from, making them a valuable source for various applications. - IMPORTANT APPLICATIONS OF CFDNA - Non-invasive prenatal testing to screen for chromosomal abnormalities in a developing fetus like Down syndrome, Edwards syndrome, etc. - Can provide information about genetic mutations and alterations present in tumor cells and early cancer detection. - Aid in diagnosis of infectious diseases and in forensics. - Used as a biomarker for neurological disorders like Alzheimer’s disease, etc. - Provide an early indication of graft rejection in patients who underwent organ transplants. - 10. VARIATIONS IN THE GENOME- IDENTITY MARKERS - Humans are 99.9% identical in genome to one another. But given the size of the human genome (3 billion base pairs), even a small (0.1%) proportion of variation is huge. - The variation in the base pairs is called polymorphism. They are very important to decide the complete makeup of an individual including one's eye color, susceptibility to disease, one’s parentage, and even ancestry, etc. - Further variations can be of many types, like single base variation, large sequence variation, variations in the way sequences are structured, etc. - Study of variations has interesting applications. - DNA Fingerprinting - - Certain sequences in the genome (15–100 bases) repeat multiple times, called VNTR (Variable Number of Tandem Repeats). - The number of repeats differs between individuals. - By counting repeats on chromosomes (from both mother and father), an individual’s unique identity can be established. - This unique pattern is called the DNA fingerprint. - Applications: Forensics, Establishing parentage. - Single Nucleotide Polymorphism (SNP) and Population Genetics (Nobel Prize 2022 related) - Single-letter changes in DNA = SNP. - Occur on average once every 300 bases in a person’s DNA. - Can result from mutations during replication or be inherited. - Nobel Prize 2022: Research on gene flow from Neanderthals and Denisovans to Homo sapiens after migration out of Africa (~70,000 years ago). - Some inherited genes from ancient relatives affect immune system responses. - Applications: - Biological markers to locate disease-associated genes. - Helps in pandemic preparedness. - Act as ancestry markers → crucial in population genetics. - Tracing gene flow using SNPs establishes ancestral links. - 1000 Genome Project - Global scientific effort to study variations in human DNA. - Includes SNPs and larger structural variations in the genome. - 11. ONE DAY ONE GENOME INITIATIVE - Initiative of: - Department of Biotechnology and Biotechnology Research and Innovation Council - It will highlight the unique bacterial species found in India, and emphasize their critical roles in environment, agriculture, and human health. - BACTERIA - Microscopic, single-celled organisms (prokaryotic microorganisms). - Bacteria inhabit the air, soil, water, acidic hot springs, radioactive waste, and the deep biosphere of Earth's crust. - BENEFICIAL USE CASES OF BACTERIA - Soil fertility: - Convert atmospheric nitrogen into plant-usable form; aid in nutrient uptake, especially phosphorus. - Food Industry: - Fermentation of various food products (curd, cheese), preservation of food; development of distinct flavors and textures; probiotics. - Biomedical use: - Used as a host organism in genetic engineering to produce various proteins, enzymes, and other products (like insulin, growth hormones, etc.). Used as vectors (vehicles) to carry therapeutic genes into human tissues. - Biofuel production: - GM bacteria and cyanobacteria can be used for biofuel production. - Biogas production: - Break down organic matter in the absence of oxygen, producing biogas (methane and carbon dioxide) as a byproduct. - Bioremediation: - Can break down a range of pollutants, including hydrocarbons and toxic chemicals to clean up contaminated environments. - 12. STEM CELLS - - Stem cells: - Undifferentiated cells with the potential to develop into specialized cell types in the body. - Types of Stem cells: - Totipotent - Most versatile stem cells with potential to differentiate into all cell types of an organism as well as extraembryonic tissues like placenta and umbilical cord. Found only in fertilized egg (zygote). - Pluripotent - Derived from inner cell mass of blastocyst. Can differentiate into all three primary germ layers (endoderm, mesoderm, ectoderm), which give rise to all body cell types except extraembryonic. Examples include embryonic stem cells and induced pluripotent stem cells reprogrammed from adult cells. - Multipotent - More specialized than pluripotent, capable of differentiating into a limited range of cell types within a specific tissue or organ. Examples include hematopoietic stem cells giving rise to various blood cells and mesenchymal stem cells differentiating into bone, cartilage, or fat cells. - Unipotent - Most specialized type, able only to self-renew and differentiate into one specific cell type. Example: skin stem cells that produce only skin cells. - 13. INDUCED-PLURIPOTENT STEM CELLS (IPSCS) - - iPSCs are adult somatic (body) cells that have been genetically reprogrammed to an embryonic stem cell–like state, giving them the ability to differentiate into almost any type of cell in the body. Scientists introduce specific genes (like Oct4, Sox2, Klf4, and c-Myc) into adult cells (e.g., skin or blood cells) to “reset” them to a pluripotent state. - If one can reprogram specialized cells (somatic cells) to tweak the transcriptional factors, it can reverse into embryonic stem cells. - Reprogramming adult cells to become pluripotent is done by introduction of four specific genes, collectively known as Yamanaka factors. It was awarded the Nobel Prize in 2012. - Advantage: - Since iPSCs can be derived directly from a patient's own cells, it eliminates the need for embryonic cells and avoids issues related to immune rejection. - iPS cells hold promise in both regenerative medicine (tissue engineering/organ transplantation) and drug development, and allow researchers to study diseases at the cellular level. - 14. STEM CELL THERAPY - - Use of stem cells to grow healthy adult cells in the lab to replace damaged, defective, or degraded adult cells in the body. - It is particularly useful when: - Cells are degraded as in case of neurological disorder with degeneration of neurons (Alzheimer’s, Parkinson’s, etc.) as neurons do not multiply. - Damage of organs due to accidents, old age, etc. - Body produces defective cells as in case of blood-related disorders like sickle cell anemia, beta thalassemia where regular blood transfusion is needed. In this case, there are 3 ways: - a. Stem cell transplantation of a healthy donor - b. Make iPS cells and grow healthy adult cells - c. Gene-edited stem cells from one's own body - As per the National Guidelines for Stem Cell Research (NGSCR) 2017, only bone marrow transplant or hematopoietic stem cell transplantation for blood disorders (including blood cancers and thalassemia) is permitted in India. - Note: - Stem cells from humans can be introduced in pigs to grow organs in them as they have a faster life cycle. - STEM CELL THERAPY IN TYPE 1 DIABETES - A woman with type 1 diabetes (T1D) in China has regained her insulin production through stem cell therapy. She received a transplantation of reprogrammed stem cells that reportedly “reversed” the disease. - Pluripotent stem cells are pre-programmed into insulin-producing cells (similar to the ones found in a healthy pancreas) and transplanted into the human body. - After transplantation, these pre-programmed cells will start producing insulin and help regulate glucose. This is a potential game-changer in the treatment of T1D. - 15. SOMATIC CELL NUCLEAR TRANSFER (SCNT) OR REPRODUCTIVE CLONING - - SCNT technology, also known as "cloning by nuclear transfer," is a technique used in the field of reproductive cloning. - Cloning involves 3 steps: - 1. Take any adult cell (skin, hair, nail, etc.) and suck out its nucleus. - 2. Take an egg cell from a donor and remove the nucleus from the egg/oocyte (enucleated). - 3. Inject the nucleus of the adult cell in the enucleated egg. You have an oocyte with somatic cell nucleus. - Oocyte cytoplasm reprograms the nucleus of the somatic cell and it makes an embryonic stem cell. - The embryonic stem cell can then be grown into an organism identical to the person from whom the adult cell was taken (since it is the nuclear DNA that codes for proteins). This is how Dolly the sheep was cloned. - Applications: - Help preserve endangered species, replicate highly valuable animals, and advance scientific research in areas such as regenerative medicine and genetic engineering. - Disadvantage: - Reproductive cloning is very inefficient as the nucleus from somatic cells resists reprogramming. - 16. TISSUE ENGINEERING/ REGENERATIVE MEDICINE/ ORGAN TRANSPLANTATION - One major challenge of organ transplantation or stem cell transplantation is finding suitable donors, as matching of HLA (human leukocyte antigen) plays a crucial role in the success of these procedures. - HLA genes encode proteins essential for the immune system to distinguish between self and non-self cells and respond to foreign substances (pathogens or transplanted tissues). - When the HLA match is absent, the immune system rejects organ transplants. HLA matches are best among same-sex siblings. Even between parents and offspring, there is no suitable HLA match. - Note: - To find healthy donors for stem cell transplantation, a database called National Stem Cell Registry is maintained in India. - 17. AUTOLOGOUS, ALLOGENIC AND XENOGENIC TRANSPLANTS - In the context of organ or stem cell transplantation, depending on its source, donor cells plantation can be classified as: -
TypeDescription
AutologousCells from one’s own body (including stem cell therapy, iPS cells, etc).
AllogenicCells from another's body but of the same species.
XenogenicCells from another species.
- With advancements in gene editing techniques, gene therapies and xenotransplantation are seen as potential alternatives to supply organs for transplantation. However, the major challenge is immune reaction from the human recipient. - 18. XENOTRANSPLANTATION - Organ transplantation from one species to another is called xenotransplantation. - NEED FOR XENOTRANSPLANTATION - While autologous is the best method (as there would not be immune rejection), making iPS cells takes a lot of time. Allogenic is good when there are suitable donors. - Given the shortage of donors with HLA match, scientists have been increasingly working on xenotransplantation, as animals like pigs with lower lifespan can be bred to grow human organs. - Gene editing in pigs to reduce immune rejection has made organ transplants from pigs to humans possible, which could offer help to thousands of people who face organ failure, disease, or injury. - Natural lifespan of a pig is 30 years, they are easily bred and can have organs of similar size to humans. - 19. MITOCHONDRIAL DNA TREATMENT - - Mitochondrial DNA is more prone to mutations compared to nuclear DNA. This is because mitochondria are exposed to free radicals generated during energy production, which can damage DNA. - When mitochondria are impaired, they do not produce sufficient energy, which affects how organs function and cause inherited conditions like heart problems, liver failure, brain disorders, blindness, and muscular dystrophy. There is no cure for mitochondrial DNA diseases at present. - MITOCHONDRIAL DONATION TREATMENT (THREE PARENT BABY) - Mitochondrial donation involves replacing unhealthy mitochondria in the mother with healthy mitochondria from a donor through in-vitro fertilisation (IVF). This would avoid passing faulty mitochondria to the child. - The Process: - The mother's egg with faulty mitochondria is collected in the laboratory. - Eggs from the donor (female) with healthy mitochondria are collected, and its nucleus is removed. - The nucleus carrying the mother's DNA is inserted into the donor’s enucleated egg. - The ‘reconstructed egg’ is fertilised with the father's sperm via IVF. - The final product — ‘resulting embryo’ — having genetic material (DNA) from parents, and healthy mitochondria from the donor, is implanted into the mother’s uterus. The baby thus produced has three genetic parents. - 20. R-DNA TECHNOLOGY - - Recombinant DNA technology or genetic engineering involves manipulation of the genetic code or DNA of living organisms. - Basic principle: - Isolating a specific gene or DNA sequence of interest from one organism and inserting it into the genome of another organism. The inserted gene can be from the same species or from a different species. - Key steps in R-DNA technology: - Isolation of the gene or DNA sequence of interest from the source organism. - Fragmenting this DNA using ‘molecular scissors’ (Restriction endonuclease Enzymes). - Screening the fragments for a ‘desired gene’. - Inserting the fragments with the desired gene into a ‘vector’ (plasmids, bacteriophage, cosmid) to develop a recombinant DNA (done using an enzyme called DNA ligase which acts like molecular glue). - Introducing the recombinant vector into the target organism or host cell. The vector integrates into the host's genome and the gene of interest is expressed. - Expression: - The target organism produces the protein encoded by the inserted gene. - APPLICATIONS OF R-DNA TECHNOLOGY - Creation of Genetically modified (GM) crops with desirable traits (resistance to pests, diseases, or herbicides). - Production of therapeutic proteins such as insulin, interferon, and human growth hormone (Human insulin was the 1st therapeutic protein to be genetically cloned in E.coli using R-DNA technology). - Creation of Mono-clonal antibodies. - Production of vaccines. E.g., Hepatitis-B vaccine. - How is a vaccine produced? - 1. Identifying the Pathogen - Scientists first identify the virus or bacteria causing the disease. - They study its structure, proteins, and genetic material to figure out which part can safely trigger an immune response. - 2. Choosing the Vaccine Type - Live-attenuated vaccines – Use a weakened form of the pathogen that cannot cause severe disease. - Inactivated vaccines – Use pathogens that are killed or inactivated so they cannot replicate. - Subunit, recombinant, or protein-based vaccines – Use only specific proteins or parts of the pathogen. - mRNA vaccines – Use genetic instructions (mRNA) for human cells to produce a harmless protein of the pathogen. - Viral vector vaccines – Use a harmless virus to deliver genetic material from the target pathogen. - 3. Growing the Pathogen (if required) - For live or inactivated vaccines, the virus or bacteria is grown in controlled conditions—often in cell cultures or eggs. - This produces enough of the pathogen or protein to make the vaccine. - 4. Inactivation or Attenuation - If needed, the pathogen is killed (inactivated) or weakened (attenuated) so it can’t cause disease but still triggers immunity. - 5. Purification - The active ingredient (virus, protein, or mRNA) is purified to remove contaminants. - 6. Formulation - Additives like stabilizers, preservatives, or adjuvants may be included: - Adjuvants boost immune response. - Stabilizers maintain effectiveness during storage. - 7. Testing and Quality Control - Each batch is tested for safety, potency, and purity before human use. - Extensive clinical trials are conducted in three phases to ensure safety and efficacy. - 8. Packaging - The vaccine is packaged in vials or pre-filled syringes and stored under the required temperature conditions. - Backbone of diagnostic tests for diseases like HIV and Hepatitis. - Produce clotting factors for treating Haemophilia. - Development of synthetic anti-venom, free from animal-derived proteins. - How is anti-venom produced in traditional way? - 1. Venom Collection - First, the venom is extracted from the snake (or scorpion, spider, etc.). This is called milking. - The collected venom is then carefully measured and sometimes diluted to make it safe for immunization purposes. - 2. Immunization of Host Animal - A large animal (commonly horse, sometimes sheep or goat) is chosen because it can produce a lot of blood/antibodies. - The animal is injected with small, non-lethal doses of venom over several weeks or months. - This stimulates the animal’s immune system to produce antibodies against the venom. - 3. Antibody Harvesting - After sufficient antibody production, blood is drawn from the animal. - The blood is processed to separate the plasma, which contains the antibodies. - 4. Purification - The antibodies are purified and concentrated. - Sometimes, they are enzymatically processed to produce F(ab')₂ fragments, which reduce allergic reactions in humans. - 5. Formulation - The purified antibodies are mixed with stabilizers, sometimes freeze-dried, and prepared for medical use. - The final product is called antivenom or antivenin, which can neutralize venom in snakebite victims. - Create genetically engineered microorganisms for bioremediation and cleaning up environmental pollutants. - CONCERNS OF R-DNA TECHNOLOGY - Environmental release of GMOs may have unintended consequences.
