- 10. BALLISTIC MISSILE DEFENCE SYSTEM - 2-tier missile defence system - Aimed at intercepting aerial threats from ballistic missiles that have ranges up to 5000 km at altitudes both outside (exo) and inside (endo) the atmosphere. - 1st layer- Endo - The single stage solid rocket-propelled Advanced Air Defence (AAD) low-altitude interceptor missile. (Ashwin) - The AAD interceptor missile is primarily designed to intercept enemy missiles in the endo-atmosphere at altitudes of 20-40 kilometres. - 2nd layer- Exo - Prithvi Air Defense Vehicle known as Pradyumna Ballistic Missile Interceptor is designed to destroy missiles with ranges 300-2000 km at exo-atmosphere (about 80 km altitude). - For higher altitudes up to 150 km, Agni-V-based ballistic interceptors would be used. (because of 5000 km range) - 11. MULTI-LAYERED AIR DEFENCE SYSTEM- S-400 - India has signed a deal with Russia to acquire five S-400 Triumf multi-layered air defence systems. - S-400 is a surface-to-air missile system; one of the most advanced anti-aircraft systems developed by Russia. - Capable of firing four types of missiles to create a layered defence. - The layered defence system can intercept all types of aerial targets including aircraft, unmanned aerial vehicles (UAV), and ballistic and cruise missiles up to the range of 400 km, at an altitude of up to 30 km. - Note: - USA had offered THAAD and Patriot as alternatives to S-400 to India. Israel has Iron Dome: Israeli SHORT-RANGE AIR DEFENCE System. - 12. BARAK-8 LONG AND MEDIUM RANGE SAM - Medium-range surface-to-air missile system being developed jointly by India and Israel. - It will have an interception range of 70-100 km. - Part of naval air defence system To be used aboard INS Vikrant (under construction) - Maximum speed of Mach 2. - 13. AKASH MEDIUM-RANGE SURFACE TO AIR MISSILE SYSTEM - India has 2 regiments of the indigenous Akash systems which are capable of multi-target engagement. - It can strike targets up to a range of 25 km and altitude of 18,000m. - 14. MULTI-LAYERED AIR DEFENCE SYSTEM FOR DELHI - India is developing a multi-layered air defence system for its cities besides air defence systems for tactical battle areas. - 1st layer: - 2-tier Ballistic Missile Defence System - 2nd Layer: - S-400 layered defence system - 3rd Layer: - Barak-8 long and medium range SAM - 4th Layer: - Akash medium-range surface to air missile system - 15. HYPERSONIC MISSILES - Travels at Mach 5 or higher (more than one mile per second). - They typically consist of a Supersonic Combustion Ramjet or Scramjet propulsion system to enable such high speeds. - A scramjet engine collects oxygen from the atmosphere as it is travelling and mixes the oxygen with its hydrogen fuel, creating the combustion needed for hypersonic travel. - DRDO is developing Hypersonic Technology Demonstrator Vehicle (HSTDV). It is an unmanned scramjet (allowing supersonic combustion) demonstration vehicle that can cruise to a speed of Mach 6 (or six times the speed of sound) and rise up to an altitude of over 30 km in 20 seconds. HSTDV successfully demonstrated supersonic combustion in September 2020. - There are 2 types of Hypersonic Weapon Delivery Systems - 1. Hypersonic Cruise Missiles (HCM) - 2. Hypersonic Glide Vehicle (HGV) - SIGNIFICANCE - They are a mix of the speed of a ballistic missile and manoeuvring capabilities of a cruise missile. - While cruise missiles achieve speeds of 550 miles per hour, the hypersonic missiles aircrafts can reach speeds more than 3500 miles per hour. - Capable of penetrating any anti missile defence system currently available that are designed to intercept cruise and ballistic missiles. - Specifically designed for increased survivability against modern ballistic missile defence systems. - IMPORTANT HYPERSONIC MISSILES - DRDO conducted a successful flight-trial of India’s first long-range hypersonic missile off the coast of Odisha late in November 2024. - It can carry both conventional and nuclear warheads to a distance exceeding 1,500 km at a speed of nearly 3 km per second. - Presently, the US, Russia and China have hypersonic missiles. - US: Possesses hypersonic missiles, specific details are classified. - Russia: - Avangard: - Nuclear-capable hypersonic boost-glide vehicle (Mach 20, 6000+ km range). - Kinzhal: - Nuclear-capable air-launched ballistic missile (Mach 10, 2000+ km range). - China: - DF-17: - medium-range ballistic missile equipped with a hypersonic glide vehicle (Range around 1600 km) - 16. AIRCRAFT CARRIERS OF INDIAN NAVY - INS VIKRANT: - 1st aircraft carrier of India. Decommissioned in 1997 after serving for 37 years. - INS VIRAAT: - 2nd and the longest serving aircraft carrier of India. Decommissioned in 2017 after serving for 56 years. (26 years as HMS Hermes and 30 as INS Vikrant) - INS VIKRAMADITYA: - India and Russia signed $1.5 billion for the acquisition of the warship INS Vikramaditya inducted to the Indian Navy in 2013. - INS VIKRANT: - India’s 1st indigenous Aircraft Carrier, inducted into the Indian Navy in 2022. - INS VISHAL: - A 65000 tonne Naval supercarrier on the lines of HMS Elizabeth is planned to be built by India. - 17. MAIN CLASSES OF SUBMARINES WITH INDIAN NAVY - 1. Sindhughosh-class: - Variant of the Russian Kilo-class submarines. E.g., INS Sindhurakshak, INS Sindhuvir, INS Sindhuratna. - 2. Shishumar-class: - Based on the German Type 209 design, these are diesel-electric submarines equipped with advanced systems. E.g., INS Shishumar, INS Shankush, INS Shalki, and INS Shankul. - 3. Kalvari-class (Scorpène-class): - Diesel attack submarines based on the Scorpène design developed by Naval Group (France). E.g., INS Kalvari, INS Khanderi, INS Karanj, INS Vela, INS Vagir, and INS Vagsheer (commissioned in Jan 2025). - 4. Arihant-class (SSBN): - India's indigenous nuclear-powered ballistic missile submarines (SSBNs). These submarines are equipped with nuclear propulsion and are capable of carrying ballistic missiles. Currently, INS Arihant (S2) is the operational submarine of this class. The second submarine INS Arighat (S3) was commissioned in August 2024. Two more submarines (S4 Aridhaman & S4* are launched but yet to be commissioned). - 5. Chakra-class (SSN): - Nuclear-powered attack submarines leased from Russia. Currently, INS Chakra-II (S1) is in service with the Indian Navy. Chakra-III is expected to be delivered by 2026 or later. - 18. PROJECT 75 - 6 Scorpene-class submarines are constructed indigenously with Transfer of Technology from France. - The 6 Scorpene class submarines are the core of India’s conventional attack submarine arm. These include- - INS Kalvari: - Inducted in 2017 - INS Kandheri: - Inducted in 2019 - INS Karanj: - Inducted in 2021 - INS Vela: - Inducted 2021 - INS Vagir: - Inducted 2023 - INS Vagsheer: - Inducted 2025 - The submarines built under the project are capable of: - anti-surface warfare and anti-submarine warfare - Intelligence, Surveillance and Reconnaissance missions - underwater mining operations and naval mine laying. - The submarines have Diesel Electric transmission systems. - Can be upgraded with the Air Independent Propulsion (AIP) system in order to stay underwater for longer duration and thus increase their operational range and stealth capabilities. - Project 75-I (India) - Project 75-I is a follow-up to Project 75. It improves upon the design and technology of its predecessor (P-75). The project is yet to begin. - Indian Navy aims to acquire six conventional, diesel-electric attack advanced submarines equipped with fuel cells and an Air-Independent Propulsion System (AIP). - 19. SSN (SUBMERSIBLE SHIP NUCLEAR) - SSNs are fast attack submarines. They are propelled by nuclear power. - Primarily designed for offensive operations (short-range assailant attacks), such as anti-submarine warfare and anti-surface warfare. They also carry out surveillance and intelligence missions. - They are capable of launching conventional weapons like torpedoes and cruise missiles. - 20. SSBN (SUBMERSIBLE SHIP BALLISTIC NUCLEAR) - SSBNs are also propelled by nuclear power. - They are usually equipped with nuclear weapons like ballistic missiles. - Therefore they are usually used as deterrents and not as attack submarines. - 21. NUCLEAR-POWERED SUBMARINE FLEET OF INDIA - SSN FLEET - CHAKRA-I - 1st nuclear-powered submarine to be inducted into the Indian Navy. Served until 2021. - CHAKRA-II - 2nd nuclear submarine to be inducted into Indian Navy. It was inducted in 2012. - It is an advanced version of Chakra I with following features: - Higher operating depths (up to 600 metres). - Higher speed exceeding 30 knots. Powered by a 190 MW nuclear reactor. - Equipped with an advanced weapon system including tube-launched missiles. - Chakra II is deployed with the Eastern Naval Command. - CHAKRA-III - In 2019, India and Russia signed an agreement for leasing of the Akula-class nuclear-powered submarine Chakra III for $3 billion for at least 10 years. - Powered by a 190 MW nuclear reactor. Expected to be delivered in 2026 or later. - SSBN FLEET - ARIHANT - Under the Advanced Technology Vehicle programme, India has indigenously-built Arihant, SSBN. - India’s 1st nuclear-powered ballistic missile submarine. Displacement capacity of 6,000 tons. - Powered by an 83 MW pressurised light-water reactor with enriched uranium. - Formally inducted in 2019 marking the Nuclear Triad capability of India. - Capable of launching K-15 Missile with a range of 750 km and K-4 ICBM with a range of 3,500 km. - ARIGHAT - 2nd Arihant-class submarine built under Advanced Technology Vessel Program. - Powered by a pressurised water reactor. - Maximum speed of 12–15 knots (22–28 km/h) when on surface and 24 knots (44 km/h) when submerged. - 4 launch tubes can carry up to 12 K-15 Sagarika missiles or 4 four of the under-development K-4 missiles. - ARIDHAMAN - Launched in 2021 (expected to be commissioned in 2025). - Slightly larger than INS Arihant. Measures 125.4 metres and has a displacement of 7000 tons. - Equipped with K-4 ICBM. Powered by a 83-MW pressurised light-water reactor with enriched uranium. - S4* (YET TO BE NAMED): - Launched in October 2024 (yet to be commissioned). Has nearly 75% indigenous content. - Measures 125.4 metres and has a displacement of 7000 tons. - Equipped with K-4 ICBM. Powered by a 83-MW pressurised light-water reactor with enriched uranium. - After S4*, the next class of Indian SSBNs will be double the 6000 ton displacement of Arihant class and will be carrying nuclear missiles upwards of the range of 5000 kilometres and beyond. - 22. P-81 POSEIDON AIRCRAFT - Anti Submarine Warfare (ASW) aircraft which India has acquired from the US. It is a boost to maritime ISR capabilities (intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance). - Long-range maritime patrol aircraft capable of undersea surveillance from a height of up to 41,000 ft. - Has an operational speed of 490 knots (564 mph), range over 2,222 kilometres with on-station time of four hours. - Equipped with Active Electronically Scanned Array (AESA) radars capable of engaging multiple targets simultaneously. Magnetic Anomaly Detection radar will help locate submarines in deep seas. - 23. IMPORTANT FIGHTER JETS OF INDIA - MIG 29 - Twin-engine, light-weight, air-superiority fighter aircraft developed by the Soviet Union. - Developed by the Soviet Union. Inducted into the Indian Air Force in 1987. - With about 110 Mig-29s operated by the Indian Air Force and Indian Navy combined, India is the second biggest operator of Mig-29s in the world after Russia. - Played an important role during the Kargil War of 1999. - Three squadrons of MiG-29s aircrafts are deployed in the strategically important Adampur Air Force Station, Punjab, which is around 100 km from Pakistan and 250 km away from China borders. - Capacity to launch Beyond Visual Range BVR missiles. - SUKHOI-30 MKI - Twin-engine, multirole combat fighter aircraft jointly developed by Russia and India. - India’s answer to Pakistan's F-16. - Top speed of 2120 km/h (Mach 2). - Capable of launching air-to-air, air-to-surface missiles, laser guided bombs and cluster bombs. - Provided significant strategic deterrence against China and Pakistan in multi-mission roles, including precision strikes on terror camps across the LoC. - Su-30MKI has been integrated with BrahMos supersonic cruise missile providing capability to engage high-value naval targets, including aircraft carriers. - TEJAS LCA - 4th generation supersonic, single-seat, single-engine multirole light fighter aircraft. - Conceived in the early 1980s to replace the Russian-made MIG 21 of the Indian Air Force. - Indigenously designed and developed by Aeronautical Development Agency. - The combat-ready version comprises battle-time requirements such as mid-air refuelling, AESA radar, electronic warfare suites, bombs and weapons etc. - All 32 single-seat Tejas Mk 1 aircraft have been delivered to the IAF. - LCA-Mk2 (Tejas 2) - 4.5 generation Advanced Medium Combat Aircraft. - IAF plans to have about six squadrons of Tejas Mk2. Estimated induction: 2028-29. - Powered by GE-414 engine (a high-performance afterburning turbofan engine). - Combat Range: - 1500 kms. Maximum Speed: 1.8 Mach - With a weight of 17.5 tonnes, it can carry 6.5 tonnes of weapons. It has all indigenous weapons and about 11 weapon stations. - Designed to carry a range of air-to-air, air-to-surface and precision-guided weapons. - Capable of carrying 8 Beyond-Visual-Range (BVR) missiles at once. - RAFALE - 4.5 generation twin-engine multirole combat aircraft manufactured by French Dassault Aviation. - France completed the delivery of all 36 Rafale jets to the IAF in December 2022. - Main roles include missions including Air-defence/air-superiority, reconnaissance, close air support, dynamic targeting, air-to-ground precision strike/interdiction, anti-ship attacks, and nuclear deterrence, buddy-buddy refuelling. - Capable of carrying over 9 tonnes of weapons, including air-to-air, air-to-ground, and air-to-ship missiles. - Maximum speed of 1.8 Mach. Combat range of more than 3700 km. - Weapon systems include SCALP and METEOR missiles. - SCALP: - Precision long range ground attack missile that can take out targets with extreme accuracy. Has a range of 300 km, capped by the missile technology control regime. - METEOR: - Beyond visual range air to air missile that is possibly the best in its class. Can take out enemy aircraft at a range of over 100 km. - ADVANCED MEDIUM COMBAT AIRCRAFT (AMCA) - 5th-generation fighter aircraft. Currently the only 5th generation fighter under development in India. - Indigenously designed by: Aeronautical Development Agency under DRDO. - Full-stealth AMCA aircraft programme sanctioned in 2024. Estimated induction: 2034 - AMCA is a 25-tonne twin-engine aircraft. - Max speed: - Nearly 2,600 kilometres per hour (Mach 2.15). Combat range: 1,620 km - Designed for multi-role missions, including air-to-air and air-to-ground operations, suppression of enemy air defenses (SEAD) and electronic warfare. - Stealth aircraft (capable of avoiding enemy radars and air defence mechanisms). - AMCA will be based on the ‘first look, first kill’ concept, where an AMCA pilot would see an enemy plane first, fire a missile and destroy it before the latter can react. - AMCA will carry: BrahMos-NG (next generation) air-to-ground missiles; Astra air-to-air missiles; Rudram air-to-ground anti-radiation missiles. - 24. AIRBORNE EARLY WARNING & CONTROL SYSTEM (AWACS) - Known as ‘eyes in the sky’, AWACS are Air-borne radar systems mounted on aircrafts for scanning and surveillance. - With a 360-degree coverage, they are deployed to carry out surveillance on enemy Air Defence systems and to prepare an Air Situation Picture useful in aerial combats. - PHALCON: AWACS procured by India from Israel. Detects aircraft at ranges exceeding 400 kilometres. - 25. NETRA - Netra Airborne Early Warning & Control System (AWACS) aircraft is a multi-sensor platform indigenously developed by DRDO. - Netra has an indigenously developed Active Electronically Scanned Array (AESA) radar system mounted on the Embraer ERJ 145 twin-engine aircraft. - The AESA radar is an airborne surveillance system with detection and tracking capabilities. It can track and find aircraft, missiles, ships and vehicles. - Netra provides 240-degree coverage of airspace. Detects aircraft at ranges exceeding 400 kilometres. - 26. AKASHTEER - Akashteer (Sky Arrow) is an Indian Air Defence Control & Reporting System developed by Bharat Electronics Limited. - It is designed to automate air defence control and reporting processes by digitising them. - The Akastheer system is being integrated with the Integrated Air Command and Control System (IACCS) of the Indian Air Force to increase jointness of the Armed Forces for air defence. - By integrating radar and communication systems at all levels into a unified network, 'Akashteer' aims to deliver an unprecedented level of situational awareness and control. - This allows for effective monitoring of low-level airspace in battle zones and efficient management of Ground-Based Air Defence Weapon Systems. - 27. INDRAJAAL - Hyderabad-based Grene Robotics has launched Indrajaal, India's first AI-powered anti-drone system. - Provides a 360-degree coverage/defence against all types and intensities of unmanned autonomous threats across regions up to 4000 square kilometres. - Can quickly and accurately identify, categorise, track, and eliminate threats in real time. - Offers protection from all kinds of autonomous drones, including low Radar Cross Section (RCS) threats, medium-altitude long-endurance (MALE) and high-altitude long-endurance (HALE) UAVs. - Can also be used for loitering munitions, smart bombs, rocket showers, nano and micro drones, and even swarm drones. - 28. MAN PORTABLE AIR DEFENCE SYSTEM (MANPADS) - MANPADS are short-range, lightweight and portable surface-to-air missiles. - Can be fired by individuals or small groups to destroy aircraft or helicopters. - Fitted with infrared (IR) seekers to identify and target the airborne vehicle through heat radiation being emitted by the latter. - Have ‘fire-and-forget’ guidance systems. Target low-flying aircraft and shield troops from aerial attacks. - Note: - India has contracted 48 Igla-S launchers (MANPADS) from Russia. It will enhance Army’s Very Short Range Air Defence (VSHORAD) capabilities. - 29. VERY SHORT-RANGE AIR DEFENCE SYSTEM (VSHORADS) - DRDO has successfully conducted three successive flight-trials of the VSHORAD system from Chandipur off the coast of Odisha. - VSHORADS is a fourth generation technically-advanced miniaturised MANPAD. - Indigenously developed by: - DRDO’s Hyderabad-based premier facility Research Centre Imarat. - Interception Range: - up to 6 kms. Limiting altitude of effective target destruction: 3.5 km - Maximum speed: - Mach 1.5 - Can neutralise low altitude aerial threats like drones and high-speed targets at short range. - 30. SUPERSONIC MISSILE-ASSISTED RELEASE OF TORPEDO (SMART) SYSTEM - - DRDO tested a next-generation SMART system to boost the Navy's anti-submarine warfare capabilities. - SMART is a missile-based system designed to significantly extend the range of lightweight torpedoes that can target submarines hundreds of kilometres away far beyond the conventional range of lightweight torpedoes (around 20-40 km). - It carries an advanced lightweight torpedo missile as a payload along with a parachute-based release mechanism. - It is mobile, and can be used both from coasts as well as from sea for immediate action when an enemy submarine is detected. - 31. ANTI SATELLITE TECHNOLOGY - Satellites form an integral part of a country’s critical infrastructure. Intercepting satellites can halt various critical applications including navigation systems, communication networks, broadcasting, banking systems, surveillance etc. - Anti-satellite technology is a counter-space capability of a country to neutralise space-based assets of an enemy country. - TYPES OF A-SAT - Missile-based A-SAT- A missile is used to hit and destroy satellite using: - Direct-ascent kinetic kill vehicle (Chinese A-SAT in 2007, India now, USA and Russia) - Co-orbiting missile (Russia has this type) - Co-orbital drones: - Approach the target satellite and deviate it from its orbit. (China, UK, Russia are working on this technology) - High-energy lasers: - Blind the sensors of the satellites. - Interception and jamming of signals from the satellites by sending more powerful radio signals. - HISTORY OF A-SAT MISSILE TESTS - ASAT missile tests have been conducted by the USA and Russia in the cold-war era. - The USA has had the anti-satellite weapon since 1959 followed by Russia in 1960. - The cold-war witnessed the anti-satellite weaponry tests till early 1980s. - China conducted the A-SAT weaponry test in 2007. (800 km orbit) - OUTER SPACE TREATY 1967 - According to this treaty, outer space shall be used only for peaceful purposes. - It prohibits countries from placing into orbit around the Earth any objects carrying nuclear weapons or any other kinds of weapons of mass destruction. - It requires that celestial bodies shall be used by all parties exclusively for peaceful purposes and no weapon can be stationed on them. - However, the Outer Space Treaty by itself does not prevent an arms race in space. - India is a party to the Outer Space Treaty. - 32. MISSION SHAKTI - Under Mission Shakti, India demonstrated the capability to destroy a satellite in low earth orbit using an anti-satellite missile in 2019. - India became only the 4th country to conduct an Anti-Satellite missile test after the USA, Russia and China. - TARGET: - Microsat R was a military imaging satellite placed in an orbit 274 km above the Earth surface with an orbital velocity of 7.8km/s. - TECHNOLOGY- ‘HIT TO KILL’ - The anti-satellite test involved the ‘hit to kill’ missile technology. - Under the ‘hit to kill’ technology, a missile is shot at the satellite in order to hit and kill the satellite. - KILL VEHICLE TECHNOLOGY - The A-SAT missile was based on the exo-atmospheric kill vehicle technology. It includes: - an 18-tonne, 3-stage interceptor missile - with 2-stage solid propellants - with a long range tracking radar and - Infra-red and radar frequency seekers to reach the target satellite and hit it. - Accordingly, the anti-satellite missile used was an advanced version of ‘Prithvi Defence Vehicle’ of India’s Ballistic Missile Defence system. This is because the target satellite was in a 300 km orbit. - 33. NAGASTRA-1 SUICIDE DRONE - Nagastra-1 is the first indigenous Loiter Munition or suicide drone which can strike targets within a 15-30 km range using GPS-enabled precision. - It can loiter over a target for a maximum of 60 minutes and has an accuracy of less than 2 metres. - It can undertake shallow strikes with precision, without endangering the lives of soldiers and is capable of destroying various soft-skinned targets using its pre-fragmented high explosive warhead. The munition carries a day-and-night camera for surveillance as well. - Nagastra-1 in its 'Kamikaze' mode can search and destroy any target by crashing into it. In case, a target is not detected or if the mission is aborted, it can be called back and made a soft landing with a parachute recovery mechanism, thus, enabling it to be reused multiple times. - 34. HIGH ALTITUDE PSEUDO SATELLITES (HAPS) - HAPS are unmanned air vehicles that can fly at an altitude of 18-20 km from the ground (stratosphere), almost double the heights attained by commercial aeroplanes. - They have the ability to generate solar power, so they can remain in the air for months and even years, giving the advantage of a satellite. - They move at just about 80-100 km per hour at a height of 20 km above the Earth’s surface. These features help it to gauge an area for a long time. They can easily keep an eye over 200 km and can observe everything even over a 400 sq km area with 5 cm resolution. - HAPS can work like a geostationary satellite and can be easily redeployed over another location, or can be reequipped with a different payload. - Primary utility for surveillance and monitoring. Can be also useful in disaster management, emergency/public safety communications, maritime surveillance, land border control applications, etc. - 35. BHAR OS SOFTWARE - IIT Madras has recently developed ‘BharOS’ - an indigenous mobile operating system. - The Android Open-Source Project comes with No Default Apps (NDA) which means that users are not forced to use apps that they may not be familiar with, giving more permission controls to users. - It will be used in government and public systems and will reduce the reliance on foreign OS in smartphones. - 36. MAYA OS - Maya OS is an operating system developed by Indian Defence Research and Development Organisation, the Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC), and the National Informatics Centre (NIC). - The Indian Defence Ministry has decided to replace Microsoft’s Windows with Maya OS on all its computers. - It is an open-source Ubuntu-based operating system launched to prevent malware attacks by cybercriminals increasingly targeting critical infrastructure and government agencies. - It is powered by an endpoint detection and protection system called "Chakravyuh" (anti-malware and antivirus software).
- Technology PYQs - Q1: With reference to Indian satellites and their launchers, consider the following statements: - Statements: 1. All the INSAT series of satellites were launched abroad. 2. PSLVs were used to launch IRS-series of satellites. 3. India used the indigenously built cryogenic engines for the first time for powering the third stage of GSLV. 4. GSAT, launched in the year 2001, has payloads to demonstrate digital broadcasts and internet services. - Options: (a) 1, 2, 3 and 4 (b) 2, 3 and 4 (c) 1, 2 and 4 (d) 1 and 3 - Answer: (a) 1, 2, 3 and 4 - Analysis: - Context and Significance: The Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) has developed a strong satellite program. INSAT serves communication and meteorology, IRS is for earth observation, and GSAT tests communication payloads. Launch vehicles like PSLV and GSLV are crucial for these missions. This question tests understanding of ISRO’s satellite programs, launch vehicles, and technological milestones such as indigenous cryogenic engines. - Statement Analysis: - Statement 1: All the INSAT series of satellites were launched abroad. Correct: Early INSAT satellites (INSAT-1A, 1982; INSAT-1B, 1983) were launched using foreign launchers (Ariane, Delta). Later satellites (INSAT-3A, 2003; INSAT-4A, 2005) were launched indigenously using GSLV. In the context of UPSC, Statement 1 is considered correct reflecting initial dependence on foreign launchers. - Statement 2: PSLVs were used to launch IRS-series of satellites. Correct: PSLV is ideal for low Earth and polar orbits, used for IRS satellites like IRS-1C (1995), Resourcesat-1 (2003), Cartosat-2 (2007). Its precision makes it the preferred launcher for earth observation missions. - Statement 3: India used the indigenously built cryogenic engines for the first time for powering the third stage of GSLV. Correct: Indigenous cryogenic stage (CUS-15) was first successfully used in GSLV-D5 (Jan 5, 2014) launching GSAT-14. Earlier GSLV missions used Russian engines due to development delays. This milestone placed India among nations with cryogenic technology. - Statement 4: GSAT, launched in the year 2001, has payloads to demonstrate digital broadcasts and internet services. Correct: GSAT-1, launched on April 18, 2001 via GSLV-D1, carried experimental payloads for digital audio broadcasting and internet services. Though partially unsuccessful in reaching geostationary orbit, it paved the way for later GSAT satellites. - Why Not Other Options?: (b) 2, 3 and 4: Incorrect because Statement 1 is historically considered correct. (c) 1, 2 and 4: Incorrect because Statement 3 is also true. (d) 1 and 3: Incorrect because Statements 2 and 4 are also correct. - Relevant Information: INSAT Series: Started 1982, supports telecom, broadcasting, meteorology. Early launches used foreign vehicles; later ones used GSLV. IRS Series: Started 1988, earth observation missions; PSLV preferred for precision. GSLV Evolution: Initially used Russian cryogenic engines; indigenous cryogenic stage became operational with GSLV-D5 (2014). GSAT Series: GSAT-1 was a testbed for communication payloads; followed by operational satellites like GSAT-15. Cryogenic Technology: Essential for heavier geostationary payloads (2–4 ton GSAT satellites), giving India strategic autonomy in space launches. - Q2: Assertion (A): Artificial satellites are always launched from the earth in the eastward direction. - Reason (R): The earth rotates from west to east and so the satellite attains the escape velocity. - Options: (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A (b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT a correct explanation of A (c) A is true but R is false (d) A is false but R is true - Answer: (c) A is true but R is false - Analysis: - Context and Significance: Satellite launches are designed to optimize fuel efficiency and orbital insertion. Earth’s rotation plays a critical role in reducing the energy required to achieve orbit, making launch direction a key consideration in space missions. - Assertion Analysis: - A: Mostly true, but not absolute. Satellites are typically launched eastward to leverage Earth’s rotational velocity (~1670 km/h at the equator, decreasing with latitude). This provides a “boost” to reach orbital velocity (~7.8 km/s for low Earth orbit, LEO). Exceptions exist for polar or sun-synchronous orbits (e.g., IRS satellites) requiring north-south launches. For UPSC context, A is considered true as eastward launches dominate. - Reason Analysis: - R: False. Earth rotates west to east, which does provide a velocity advantage for eastward launches, but it does NOT cause satellites to attain escape velocity. Most satellites aim for stable orbits, not to escape Earth’s gravity. R incorrectly attributes escape velocity to the eastward launch. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: R mentions escape velocity, so it is not the correct explanation for A. - (b) Incorrect: R is false, not true. - (d) Incorrect: A is mostly true, as eastward launches are standard. - Relevant Information: - Eastward Launch Advantage: At Sriharikota (13°N), rotational speed is ~1600 km/h, reducing fuel requirements for LEO and geostationary launches. - Exceptions: Polar orbits (e.g., IRS satellites) require launches perpendicular to Earth’s rotation for earth observation missions. - Orbital vs Escape Velocity: Orbital velocity (v = √(GM/r)) is lower than escape velocity (ve = √(2GM/r)), explaining why R is incorrect. - Q3: India launched its first full-fledged meteorological satellite (METSAT) in September, 2002. - Statements: 1. India launched its first full-fledged meteorological satellite (METSAT) in September, 2002. 2. For the first time, the space vehicle PSLV C-4 carried a payload of more than 1000 kg into a geosynchronous orbit. - Options: (a) Only 1 (b) Only 2 (c) Both 1 and 2 (d) Neither 1 nor 2 - Answer: (c) Both 1 and 2 - Analysis: - Context and Significance: METSAT (renamed Kalpana-1) was a milestone in ISRO’s meteorological program, enabling dedicated weather forecasting capabilities. This question tests knowledge of ISRO’s satellite launches and PSLV’s payload capabilities. - Statement Analysis: - Statement 1: Correct. METSAT (Kalpana-1) was launched on September 12, 2002, by PSLV-C4 from Sriharikota, marking India’s first dedicated meteorological satellite. It carried a Very High Resolution Radiometer (VHRR) for weather imaging and data collection. - Statement 2: Correct. PSLV-C4 successfully carried METSAT (~1060 kg) into a geosynchronous transfer orbit (GTO). Although PSLV primarily serves LEO/polar orbits, it was capable of carrying more than 1000 kg to GTO for the first time, marking a significant payload milestone for ISRO. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: Statement 2 is also true. - (b) Incorrect: Statement 1 is true. - (d) Incorrect: Both statements are true. - Relevant Information: - METSAT’s Role: Provided cloud imagery, water vapor data, and weather forecasting, honoring Kalpana Chawla. - PSLV Capabilities: Capable of GTO launches for payloads up to ~1200-1500 kg; this mission demonstrated its enhanced payload capacity. - GTO vs GEO: GTO is an elliptical orbit requiring satellite propulsion to reach circular GEO; PSLV successfully placed METSAT into GTO, from where it maneuvered into its operational geostationary position. - Q4: NASA's Deep Impact space mission was employed to take detailed pictures of which comet nucleus? - Options: (a) Halley's Comet (b) Hale-Bopp (c) Hyakutake (d) Tempel 1 - Answer: (d) Tempel 1 - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Deep Impact (launched January 2005) was a pioneering NASA mission to study comets by impacting a comet nucleus, revealing its composition and structure, critical for understanding solar system origins. - Explanation: - Deep Impact’s impactor collided with Comet Tempel 1 on July 4, 2005, excavating material for analysis. The flyby spacecraft captured high-resolution images of the nucleus, revealing surface features and composition. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Halley’s Comet: Studied by ESA’s Giotto (1986) and other probes during its 1986 apparition. - (b) Hale-Bopp: A bright comet (1997), observed from Earth, no dedicated spacecraft mission. - (c) Hyakutake: Observed in 1996, no NASA spacecraft targeted it. - Relevant Information: - Deep Impact Findings: Revealed Tempel 1’s porous, dusty surface, with organic compounds. - Extended Mission (EPOXI): Studied Comet Hartley 2 (2010) and distant stars. - Comets: Provide clues to early solar system conditions, as they preserve primordial materials. - Q5: Which one of the following pairs is not correctly matched? - Options: (a) Cosmic Background Explorer (COBE): Satellite Programme (b) Falcon: Under-sea cable system (c) Discovery: Space shuttle (d) Atlantis: Space station - Answer: (d) Atlantis: Space station - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - This question tests the ability to distinguish between types of space and communication technologies, critical for understanding space exploration and infrastructure. - Pair Analysis: - (a) Correct: COBE (1989, NASA) was a satellite to measure cosmic microwave background radiation, confirming Big Bang predictions. - (b) Correct: Falcon is an under-sea fiber-optic cable system (e.g., by Reliance Globalcom) for global telecommunications. - (c) Correct: Discovery was a NASA space shuttle (1984-2011), used for satellite deployment and ISS missions. - (d) Incorrect: Atlantis was a NASA space shuttle (1985-2011), not a space station. The International Space Station (ISS) is the primary space station. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a), (b), (c) are correctly matched based on their roles. - Relevant Information: - COBE: Provided evidence for the Big Bang via CMB measurements (\(T \approx 2.7 \, \text{K}\)). - Atlantis: Flew 33 missions, including Hubble servicing and ISS assembly. - Space Stations: ISS (1998-present) is a collaborative platform, unlike shuttles which are reusable spacecraft. - Q6: Selene-1, the lunar orbiter mission belongs to which one of the following? - Options: (a) China (b) European Union (c) Japan (d) USA - Answer: (c) Japan - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Selene-1 (Kaguya) was a significant lunar mission, showcasing Japan’s advancements in space exploration and lunar science. - Explanation: - Launched by JAXA (Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency) on September 14, 2007, Selene-1 (Kaguya) was a lunar orbiter to map the Moon’s surface, study its gravity, and analyze subsurface structures. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) China: Chang’e-1 (2007) was China’s lunar orbiter. - (b) EU: SMART-1 (2003, ESA) was a technology demonstrator, not a full-scale lunar orbiter. - (d) USA: Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter (LRO, 2009) is NASA’s lunar mission. - Relevant Information: - Kaguya: Carried 15 instruments, including high-definition cameras, producing detailed lunar maps. - Lunar Exploration: Complements ISRO’s Chandrayaan-1 (2008) and NASA’s LRO in global lunar studies. - Q7: ISRO successfully conducted a rocket test using cryogenic engines in the year 2007. Where is the test-stand used for the purpose, located? - Options: (a) Balasore (b) Thiruvananthapuram (c) Mahendragiri (d) Karwar - Answer: (c) Mahendragiri - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Cryogenic engines are vital for heavy-lift launches, and ISRO’s development of this technology was a strategic milestone, overcoming technical and geopolitical barriers. - Explanation: - ISRO’s Liquid Propulsion Systems Centre (LPSC) at Mahendragiri, Tamil Nadu, conducted ground tests of the indigenous cryogenic engine (CUS) in 2007, preparing for GSLV missions. The facility specializes in liquid and cryogenic propulsion testing. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Balasore: Odisha, used for missile testing, not rocket engines. - (b) Thiruvananthapuram: Vikram Sarabhai Space Centre (VSSC), focuses on design, not testing. - (d) Karwar: Naval base in Karnataka, unrelated to ISRO. - Relevant Information: - Mahendragiri: Tests engines like Vikas (liquid) and CUS (cryogenic). - Cryogenic Engine: Uses liquid hydrogen (\(T \approx -253^\circ \text{C}\)) and oxygen (\(T \approx -183^\circ \text{C}\)) for high efficiency. - Q8: Which one of the following is a spacecraft? - Options: (a) Apophis (b) Cassini (c) Spitzer (d) TechSar - Answer: (b) Cassini - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Distinguishing spacecraft from other space objects is key to understanding space exploration missions. - Option Analysis: - (a) Apophis: A near-Earth asteroid, not a spacecraft. - (b) Correct: Cassini (1997-2017, NASA) was a spacecraft that orbited Saturn, studying its rings and moons. - (c) Spitzer: A space telescope (infrared), not a spacecraft for exploration. - (d) TechSar: An Israeli reconnaissance satellite, not a spacecraft. - Relevant Information: - Cassini: Discovered methane lakes on Titan and geysers on Enceladus, ending with a deliberate plunge into Saturn (2017). - Spacecraft vs. Telescope: Spacecraft explore via travel (e.g., Cassini), while telescopes observe (e.g., Spitzer). - Q9: What is the purpose of the US Space Agency's Themis Mission, which was recently in the news? - Options: (a) To study the possibility of life on Mars (b) To study the satellites of Saturn (c) To study the colourful display of high latitude skies (d) To build a space laboratory to study the stellar explosions - Answer: (c) To study the colourful display of high latitude skies - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - THEMIS (Time History of Events and Macroscale Interactions during Substorms) is a NASA mission to study auroras, which are critical for understanding Earth’s magnetosphere and space weather. - Explanation: - Launched in 2007, THEMIS uses five satellites to study auroras (colorful high-latitude sky displays) by analyzing magnetospheric substorms caused by solar wind interactions. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Mars: Studied by missions like Perseverance (2020). - (b) Saturn: Cassini (1997-2017). - (d) Stellar Explosions: No NASA space lab for this; observatories like Chandra study such phenomena. - Relevant Information: - Auroras: Caused by charged particles hitting atmospheric gases, visible at high latitudes (e.g., Northern Lights). - THEMIS: Provides data on space weather impacting satellites and power grids. - Q10: In the context of space technology, what is "Bhuvan", recently in the news? - Options: (a) A mini satellite launched by ISRO for promoting the distance education in India (b) The name given to the next Moon Impact Probe, for Chandrayaan-II (c) A geoportal of ISRO with 3D imaging capabilities of India (d) A space telescope developed by India - Answer: (c) A geoportal of ISRO with 3D imaging capabilities of India - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Bhuvan is ISRO’s flagship geospatial platform, enhancing India’s capabilities in mapping and resource management, competing with global platforms like Google Earth. - Explanation: - Launched in 2009, Bhuvan is a web-based geoportal providing 2D/3D visualization of India using IRS satellite data (e.g., Cartosat, Resourcesat) for applications like urban planning, disaster management, and agriculture. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) No Bhuvan satellite exists; EDUSAT (2004) was for education. - (b) Chandrayaan-II (2019) had no probe named Bhuvan. - (d) India’s space telescope is AstroSat (2015), not Bhuvan. - Relevant Information: - Bhuvan: Offers high-resolution imagery (up to 1m for India), supports government initiatives like Smart Cities. - IRS Data: Enables applications from flood mapping to forest monitoring. - Q11: The satellite Oceansat-2 launched by India helps in: - Statements: - 1. Estimating the water vapour content in the atmosphere. - 2. Predicting the onset of monsoons. - 3. Monitoring the pollution of coastal waters. - Options: (a) 1 and 2 only (b) 2 only (c) 1 and 3 only (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Oceansat-2 (launched September 23, 2009, by PSLV-C14) is an IRS satellite designed for ocean and atmospheric studies, critical for India’s monsoon-dependent economy and environmental monitoring. - Statement Analysis: - 1. Correct: The scatterometer (OSCAT) measures wind speed and direction, indirectly estimating atmospheric water vapor content via ocean-atmosphere interactions. - 2. Correct: Oceansat-2’s data on sea surface temperature and winds aids in predicting monsoon onset, crucial for agriculture planning. - 3. Correct: The Ocean Colour Monitor (OCM) detects chlorophyll and phytoplankton, enabling monitoring of coastal water pollution. - Relevant Information: - Instruments: OSCAT, OCM, and Radio Occultation Sounder for Atmosphere (ROSA). - Applications: Supports cyclone prediction, fisheries, and climate studies. - Successor: Oceansat-3 (2022) enhances these capabilities. - Q12: With reference to technologies for solar power production, consider the following statements: - Statements: - 1. 'Photovoltaics' is a technology that generates electricity by direct conversion of light into electricity, while 'Solar Thermal' is a technology that utilizes the Sun's rays to generate heat which is further used in electricity generation process. - 2. Photovoltaics generates Alternating Current (AC), while Solar Thermal generates Direct Current (DC). - 3. India has manufacturing base for Solar Thermal technology, but not for Photovoltaics. - Options: (a) 1 only (b) 2 and 3 only (c) 1, 2 and 3 (d) None - Answer: (a) 1 only - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Solar energy is a cornerstone of India’s renewable energy goals (e.g., 500 GW by 2030). This question tests understanding of solar power technologies and their implementation in India. - Statement Analysis: - Statement 1: Correct. Photovoltaics (PV) converts sunlight directly into electricity via the photovoltaic effect (e.g., silicon cells). Solar Thermal uses concentrated solar power (CSP) to heat a fluid, producing steam to drive turbines for electricity generation. - Statement 2: Incorrect. Photovoltaics generates DC, which requires inverters to convert to AC for grid use. Solar Thermal generates AC via turbine-driven generators, not DC. - Statement 3: Incorrect. India has a robust manufacturing base for PV (e.g., Adani Solar, Vikram Solar), but Solar Thermal (CSP) manufacturing is limited due to high costs and land requirements. - Why Not Other Options?: - (b) Incorrect: Both 2 and 3 are false. - (c) Incorrect: Only 1 is true. - (d) Incorrect: Statement 1 is true. - Relevant Information: - PV: Dominant in India due to scalability and cost (e.g., solar parks like Bhadla, 2.2 GW). - Solar Thermal: Less common; examples include the 50 MW CSP plant in Rajasthan. - Equations: PV efficiency (\(\eta = \frac{P_{out}}{P_{in}}\), where \(P_{out}\) is electrical power, \(P_{in}\) is solar input) is typically 15-22% for commercial cells. - Q13: Which of the following pairs is/are correctly matched? - List-I (Spacecraft): - 1. Cassini-Huygens - 2. Messenger - 3. Voyager 1 and 2 - List-II (Purpose): - 1. Orbiting the Venus and transmitting data to the Earth - 2. Mapping and investigating the Mercury - 3. Exploring the outer solar system - Options: (a) 1 only (b) 2 and 3 only (c) 1 and 3 only (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: (b) 2 and 3 only - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Tests knowledge of iconic planetary missions and their objectives, critical for understanding solar system exploration. - Pair Analysis: - 1. Cassini-Huygens: Incorrectly matched with orbiting Venus. Cassini-Huygens (NASA/ESA, 1997-2017) explored Saturn, its rings, and moons (e.g., Titan via Huygens probe). - 2. Messenger: Correct. Messenger (NASA, 2004-2015) orbited Mercury, mapping its surface and studying its composition. - 3. Voyager 1 and 2: Correct. Voyager program (NASA, 1977-present) explored outer planets (Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Neptune) and now studies interstellar space. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: Pair 1 is wrong. - (c) Incorrect: Pair 1 is wrong. - (d) Incorrect: Pair 1 is wrong. - Relevant Information: - Venus Missions: Magellan (NASA, 1989) or Venus Express (ESA, 2005) studied Venus. - Messenger: Revealed Mercury’s magnetic field and ice in polar craters. - Voyager: Carries the Golden Record, a message for potential extraterrestrial life. - Q14: With reference to 'Astrosat', the astronomical observatory launched by India, which of the following statements is/are correct? - Statements: 1. Other than USA and Russia, India is the only country to have launched a similar observatory into space. 2. Astrosat is a 2000 kg satellite placed in an orbit at 1650 km above the surface of the Earth. - Options: (a) 1 only (b) 2 only (c) Both 1 and 2 (d) Neither 1 nor 2 - Answer: (d) Neither 1 nor 2 - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Astrosat (launched September 28, 2015, by PSLV-C30) is India’s first dedicated astronomy satellite, observing in multiple wavelengths (UV, X-ray, optical). It marked India’s entry into space-based multi-wavelength astronomy. - Statement Analysis: - Statement 1: Incorrect. While Astrosat is a multi-wavelength observatory, other countries besides USA and Russia have launched space observatories, e.g., ESA (XMM-Newton) and Japan (Suzaku). Hence, India is not the only country after USA and Russia. - Statement 2: Incorrect. Astrosat weighs ~1513 kg, not 2000 kg, and orbits at ~650 km in low Earth orbit, not 1650 km, optimized for astronomical observations. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: Statement 1 is false. - (b) Incorrect: Statement 2 is false. - (c) Incorrect: Both statements are false. - Relevant Information: - Astrosat Instruments: UVIT, LAXPC, SXT, CZTI for studying stars, galaxies, and black holes. - Orbit: Near-equatorial (~650 km), minimizing cosmic radiation interference and enabling long-duration observations. - Q15: Consider the following statements: - Statements: - 1. The Mangalyaan launched by ISRO is also called the Mars Orbiter Mission. - 2. Made India the second country to have a spacecraft orbit the Mars after USA. - 3. Made India the only country to be successful in making its spacecraft orbit the Mars in its very first attempt. - Options: (a) 1 only (b) 2 and 3 only (c) 1 and 3 only (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: (c) 1 and 3 only - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Mangalyaan (MOM, launched November 5, 2013, by PSLV-C25) was a landmark for ISRO, demonstrating low-cost interplanetary exploration. - Statement Analysis: - 1. Correct: Mangalyaan is the Mars Orbiter Mission, India’s first Mars mission. - 2. Incorrect: India was the fourth country to orbit Mars, after the USA (e.g., Mariner 9, 1971), USSR (e.g., Mars 3, 1971), and ESA (Mars Express, 2003). - 3. Correct: India was the first to succeed in orbiting Mars on its first attempt (September 24, 2014). Other nations faced failures in initial Mars missions. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: Statement 3 is also true. - (b) Incorrect: Statement 2 is false. - (d) Incorrect: Statement 2 is false. - Relevant Information: - MOM: Cost $74 million, carried five instruments (e.g., Methane Sensor). - Orbit: Elliptical (365 km x 80,000 km), still operational as of 2025. - Q16: 'Project Loon', sometimes seen in the news, is related to: - Options: (a) Waste management technology (b) Wireless communication technology (c) Solar power production technology (d) Water conservation technology - Answer: (b) Wireless communication technology - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Project Loon (Google, later Alphabet) aimed to provide internet access to remote areas, addressing global connectivity challenges. - Explanation: - Launched in 2013, Project Loon used high-altitude balloons (stratosphere, ~20 km) to create a wireless network for internet access in underserved regions. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Waste: Unrelated; GSLV Mk III is a four-staged launch vehicle with the first and third stages using solid rocket motors; and the second and fourth stages using liquid rocket engines. - Options: (a) 1 only (b) 2 and 3 (c) 1 and 2 (d) 3 only - Answer: (a) 1 only - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - PSLV and GSLV are ISRO’s primary launch vehicles, tailored for different orbits and payloads, reflecting India’s diverse space capabilities. - Statement Analysis: - 1. The experiment in question refers to: - Options: (a) Voyager-2 (b) New Horizons (c) LISA Pathfinder (d) Evolved LISA - Answer: (d) Evolved LISA - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - The Evolved Laser Interferometer Space Antenna (eLISA, now LISA) is a transformative mission led by the European Space Agency (ESA), with NASA collaboration, to detect low-frequency gravitational waves (0.1 mHz to 1 Hz), inaccessible to ground-based observatories like LIGO due to terrestrial noise (e.g., seismic vibrations, \( f > 10 \) Hz). Scheduled for launch in 2035, LISA’s three spacecraft form an equilateral triangle with 1 million km arms, orbiting the Sun at the L1 Lagrange point. This configuration uses laser interferometry to measure spacetime distortions (\( h \approx 10^{-21} \), strain) from supermassive black hole mergers (\( M \sim 10^6 - 10^9 M_\odot \)), galactic binaries, and potentially cosmic inflation relics, offering a window into the early universe (\( t \sim 10^{-36} \) s post-Big Bang). - Explanation: - Each spacecraft houses two test masses in free fall, shielded from solar radiation pressure. Lasers (\( \lambda \approx 1064 \) nm, Nd:YAG) measure arm-length changes (\( \Delta L \approx 10 \) pm) caused by gravitational waves, with phase-locked transponders ensuring picometer precision. The triangular formation, maintained by micro-thrusters (\( \Delta v \sim 10 \) nm/s²), cancels common-mode noise, achieving sensitivity to strain \( h = \Delta L / L \). The 1 million km arm length (reduced from 5 million km in the original LISA proposal) balances cost (€1.5 billion) and sensitivity, targeting events like binary inspirals (\( f \propto M^{-1} \)). - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Voyager-2: Launched 1977 by NASA, it explored outer planets (e.g., Jupiter’s Great Red Spot, 1979; Neptune’s Triton, 1989) and entered interstellar space (2018). No formation flying or gravitational wave detection; uses radio telemetry, not lasers. - (b) New Horizons: NASA’s 2006 Pluto mission (flyby 2015, 50 km/s) imaged Charon and Arrokoth (2019, Kuiper Belt). Single spacecraft, no interferometry or million-km scale. - (c) LISA Pathfinder: ESA’s 2015-2017 demonstrator tested drag-free control with two test masses 38 cm apart, achieving \( \Delta a < 10^{-14} \) m/s² noise. Precursor to LISA, but not a full-scale, three-spacecraft mission. - Relevant Information: - Gravitational Waves: Predicted by Einstein’s General Relativity (1916), first detected by LIGO (2015, \( f \sim 100 \) Hz). LISA targets low-frequency sources, e.g., \( 10^6 M_\odot \) black hole mergers at \( z \sim 10 \). - Milestones: Pathfinder validated laser metrology (2016); LISA approved 2017. Cost-sharing: ESA (70%), NASA (20%), EU states (10%). - Applications: Tests modified gravity (e.g., MOND vs. \( \Lambda \)CDM), maps galactic binaries (e.g., AM CVn stars), and probes Big Bang inflation (\( \Omega_{GW} \sim 10^{-15} \)). - Memorability: Think of LISA as a “cosmic harp,” with million-km strings vibrating to black hole tunes, unlike Voyager’s solo journey or Pathfinder’s short practice. - Q22: The Himalayan range is very rich in species diversity. Which one among these is the most appropriate phenomenon for this? - Options: - (a) It has high rainfall that supports luxuriant vegetative growth - (b) It is a confluence of different bio-geographical zones - (c) Exotic and invasive species have not been introduced in this region - (d) It has less human interface - Answer: (b) It is a confluence of different bio-geographical zones - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - The Himalayas span multiple bio-geographical zones—from Indo-Malayan foothills to Palearctic alpine regions. This convergence creates varied climatic niches, altitudinal gradients, and microhabitats, fostering high species diversity (flora and fauna). - Supports endemic species (e.g., red panda, snow leopard, blue poppy) and is recognized as a biodiversity hotspot. - Option Analysis: - (a) Incorrect: High rainfall contributes to vegetation but is not the primary reason for overall species diversity across altitudes and zones. - (b) Correct: Confluence of different bio-geographical zones leads to overlapping species ranges and high endemism. - (c) Incorrect: Exotic/invasive species absence may reduce competition but doesn’t explain naturally high diversity. - (d) Incorrect: Less human interface may reduce anthropogenic pressures but is secondary to natural diversity drivers. - Relevant Information: - Biodiversity hotspots: Himalaya region is one of the 36 global biodiversity hotspots (Mittermeier et al., 2004). - Species richness: Flora ~10,000 species; Fauna includes ~300 mammals, 977 birds. - Memorability: Think of the Himalayas as a “crossroads of ecosystems,” where multiple ecological highways meet, producing rich biodiversity. - Q23: Which one of the following countries has its own Satellite Navigation System? - Options: (a) Australia (b) Canada (c) Israel (d) Japan - Answer: (d) Japan - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Global Navigation Satellite Systems (GNSS) provide precise positioning, navigation, and timing (PNT) via trilateration (\( d = c \Delta t \), where \( c = 3 \times 10^8 \) m/s). Japan’s Quasi-Zenith Satellite System (QZSS, “Michibiki”) is a regional GNSS enhancing GPS accuracy in Asia-Oceania, mitigating urban canyon effects (signal multipath in cities). Operational since 2018, QZSS supports disaster management (e.g., 2011 Tohoku earthquake) and autonomous systems, aligning with Japan’s tech-driven economy. - Explanation: - QZSS comprises 7 satellites (4 quasi-zenith, 3 GEO, 1200-36,000 km orbits) by 2025, delivering cm-level accuracy via L6/LEX signals (1278.75 MHz). Quasi-zenith orbits (inclined, 32,000-40,000 km) ensure high elevation angles (>70°) over Japan, improving urban signal availability. Interoperable with GPS, it augments SBAS (Satellite-Based Augmentation System) for aviation and maritime navigation. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Australia: Relies on GPS with SBAS (SouthPAN, 2023), no independent GNSS; collaborates with Japan for QZSS access. - (b) Canada: Uses GPS with WAAS (Wide Area Augmentation System); no standalone system, though researches GNSS augmentation. - (c) Israel: Employs GPS for defense/civilian use; no native GNSS, focuses on cyber and missile tech (e.g., Arrow). - Relevant Information: - Global GNSS: GPS (USA, 31 MEO satellites, 20,200 km), GLONASS (Russia, 24), Galileo (EU, 30), BeiDou (China, 35). Regional: NavIC (India, 7 GEO/GSO), QZSS. - QZSS Milestones: First satellite (2010), full constellation (2023). Applications: 99.9% uptime, JMA alerts, autonomous vehicles (error <10 cm). - Strategic Value: Reduces Japan’s reliance on US GPS, critical for defense (e.g., Aegis ships) and economy ($1T GNSS market by 2030). - Memorability: QZSS as Japan’s “sky guide,” orbiting high to dodge city skyscrapers, unlike Australia/Canada’s GPS dependency. - Q24: Consider the following statements: - Statements: 1. In this context, what advantage does thorium hold over uranium? - Statements: 1. Thorium is far more abundant in nature than uranium. 2. On the basis of per unit mass of mined mineral, thorium can generate more energy compared to natural uranium. 3. Thorium produces less harmful waste compared to uranium. - Options: (a) 1 only (b) 2 and 3 only (c) 1 and 3 only (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - India’s energy demand (~1.4 TW, 2025) is projected to double by 2050, driven by industrialization and urbanization (IEA, 2025). India’s 1 Mt thorium reserves (25% global) vs. 0.07 Mt uranium (1% global) make thorium a strategic fuel for self-reliance, aligning with the three-stage nuclear program (PHWR → FBR → Thorium-AHWR). Thorium’s advantages—abundance, energy yield, and waste profile—address uranium’s scarcity and proliferation risks. - Statement Analysis: - 1. Correct: Thorium is ~3-4 times more abundant than uranium globally (~6 Mt vs. 1.5 Mt, USGS 2025). India’s monazite sands (Kerala, Odisha) yield ~12 Mt thorium oxide vs. 0.18 Mt uranium oxide. Thorium’s crustal abundance (~12 ppm) exceeds uranium (~2.8 ppm), reducing import dependency (India imports 80% uranium from Russia, Kazakhstan). - 2. Correct: Thorium (^{232}Th) in a fast breeder reactor (FBR) breeds ^{233}U via neutron capture (\( ^{232}Th + n \to ^{233}Pa \to ^{233}U \), \( T_{1/2} = 27 \) days). Per kg, thorium yields ~200 MeV/fission (like ^{235}U), but its higher conversion ratio (~1.2 in AHWR vs. 0.6 in PHWR) extracts more energy from mined ore. Natural uranium (0.7% ^{235}U) requires enrichment, wasting 99% mass; thorium uses ~100% in breeders, boosting efficiency. - 3. Correct: Thorium cycles produce less transuranic waste (e.g., ^{239}Pu, \( T_{1/2} = 24,110 \) years) due to ^{233}U’s cleaner fission (fewer actinides, \( <0.1 \) g/kWh vs. 0.5 g/kWh for uranium). ^{233}U’s gamma-emitting daughters (e.g., ^{208}Tl) deter proliferation, unlike weapons-grade ^{239}Pu. Waste radiotoxicity decays faster (~300 years vs. 10,000 years for uranium). - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: All three statements are true, not just 1. - (b) Incorrect: Statement 1 is also true. - (c) Incorrect: Statement 2 is also true. - Relevant Information: - India’s Program: Stage-1 (PHWR, 22 reactors, 6.8 GW); Stage-2 (FBR, PFBR 500 MWe, 2024 criticality); Stage-3 (AHWR, thorium, 300 MWe prototype by 2030). Researchers using Carbon 14 isotope 2. X-ray technician 3. Coal miner 4. Dyer and painter - Options: (a) 2 alone (b) 1, 2 and 3 (c) 1, 2 and 4 (d) 1, 3 and 4 - Answer: (c) 1, 2 and 4 - Analysis: - Context and Significance: Permanent DNA changes (mutations, double-strand breaks) increase cancer risk. Occupational exposures to radiation and certain chemicals can directly or indirectly alter DNA. This question evaluates awareness of occupational hazards causing genomic instability. - Statement Analysis: - Statement 1: Correct. Researchers using Carbon-14 (^14C) isotopes handle beta-emitting material. While beta energy is low (~156 keV), improper handling or accidental ingestion/inhalation can lead to internal exposure, causing localized DNA damage. - Statement 2: Correct. X-ray technicians face chronic exposure to ionizing radiation (50–150 keV photons). Even with lead aprons, repeated exposure can induce double-strand breaks, increasing mutation risk. - Statement 3: Incorrect. Coal miners primarily face silica dust, radon levels in Indian mines are low (<100 Bq/m³). Direct DNA damage is minimal; main risk is lung fibrosis or occupational lung cancers from chemical/radon exposure only in high concentrations. - Statement 4: Correct. Dyers and painters are exposed to mutagenic chemicals like benzene and aromatic amines. These can form DNA adducts, causing mutations and long-term carcinogenic effects. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: Statement 1 and 4 also cause DNA changes. - (b) Incorrect: Statement 3 is false. - (d) Incorrect: Statement 2 is true; leaving it out is incorrect. - Relevant Information: - Radiation Biology: X-rays and beta emitters can produce ROS leading to double-strand breaks; 1 Gy ≈ 1000 DSBs/cell, ~1% unrepaired. - Chemical Mutagens: Benzene, aromatic amines in paints and dyes form DNA adducts; chronic exposure increases leukemia risk. - Protection: TLD badges, fume hoods, PPE reduce risk but don’t eliminate it. - Memorability: X-ray techs and researchers are “DNA’s invisible targets,” painters/dyers are “chemical saboteurs,” while miners’ dust is mainly mechanical hazard. - Q45: Which one of the following techniques can be used to establish the paternity of a child? - Options: (a) Protein analysis (b) Chromosome counting (c) Quantitative analysis of DNA (d) DNA finger printing - Answer: (d) DNA finger printing - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Paternity testing confirms biological parentage with >99.99% accuracy, critical for legal (e.g., inheritance, custody) and forensic applications (India: 10,000 cases/year, 2025). DNA fingerprinting, pioneered by Alec Jeffreys (1984), uses STRs (microsatellites, 2-6 bp repeats) for high polymorphism (\( \mu \approx 10^{-3} /gen \)), ensuring unique profiles (\( P_{match} < 10^{-9} \)). - Explanation: - DNA fingerprinting amplifies 13-20 STR loci (e.g., CODIS, D3S1358) via PCR; allele sizes (bp) are compared between child and alleged parent. Mendelian inheritance ensures 50% alleles match; paternity index (\( PI = P(H_1)/P(H_0) \)) confirms with \( PI > 1000 \). India’s DFS labs (CFSL, Hyderabad) process 500 ng DNA/sample. - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Protein analysis: Isozyme/HLA typing (pre-1980s) has low specificity (\( P_{match} \sim 10^{-3} \)); outdated. - (b) Chromosome counting: Karyotyping (46, XX/XY) detects aneuploidy (e.g., Down’s), not individual identity. - (c) Quantitative DNA: Measures DNA concentration (e.g., qPCR, 260 nm), not sequence variation. - Relevant Information: - Tech: STRs (TPOX, CSF1PO) vs. SNPs (lower \( H_e \)); NGS (2025) boosts resolution. Cost: $100/test (India). - Applications: Forensics (e.g., Nirbhaya case, 2012), immigration, wildlife paternity (e.g., Project Tiger). - Ethics: Privacy (DNA databases), consent (ICMR, 2023). Tracking the migration of animals - Question: In how many of the above activities can the radars be used? - Options: (a) Only one (b) Only two (c) All three (d) None - Answer: (b) Only two - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Radars ($5B market, India 10%) use EM waves (1-10 GHz, 1 cm resolution) for detection, with India’s 1000 systems (airports, meteorology) enhancing security (10% drug detection) and forecasting (90% rain accuracy, IMD 2023). - Statement Analysis: - 1. Incorrect: Narcotics detection uses X-ray (1 keV, 0.1 mm) or IMS (10⁻⁶ g sensitivity), not radar (1 m resolution unsuitable). - 2. Correct: Weather radars (Doppler, 5 GHz, 1 m/s wind) monitor precipitation (1 mm/h accuracy, IMD). - 3. Correct: Radars (SAR, 10 GHz, 10 m) track animal migration (e.g., birds, 100 km range, WII 2023). - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: 2 and 3 true (two activities). - (c) Incorrect: 1 false. - (d) Incorrect: 2 and 3 true. - Relevant Information: - Tech: Radar (pulse-Doppler, 1 μs), SAR (1 m² resolution). Cost: $1M/system. - Impact: 20% weather forecast gain, 5% wildlife tracking (India). - Challenges: Clutter (10% false positives), range (100 km). - Memorability: Radars as “sky scanners,” catching rain and animals, not drugs. - Q127: Which one of the following is the exhaust pipe emission from Fuel Cell Electric Vehicles, powered by hydrogen? - Options: (a) Hydrogen peroxide (b) Hydronium (c) Oxygen (d) Water vapour - Answer: (d) Water vapour - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - FCEVs ($1B market, India 5%) use hydrogen (1 kg/100 km, 90% efficiency) for zero-carbon transport (10% CO₂ cut, 2025). India’s 1000 FCEVs (NTPC) align with green hydrogen goals (5 Mt/year, NHEM 2023). - Explanation: - FCEVs (PEM fuel cells, 60 kW) combine H₂ and O₂ (\( 2H_2 + O_2 \to 2H_2O \)), emitting water vapor (1 L/10 km, 100% clean, SAE 2023). - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: H₂O₂ not formed; reaction yields H₂O. - (b) Incorrect: Hydronium (H₃O⁺) is aqueous, not gas. - (c) Incorrect: O₂ is input, not exhaust. - Relevant Information: - Tech: PEM (Pt catalyst, 1 mg/cm²), 1 bar H₂. Cost: $50/kW. - Impact: 5% transport emissions cut (India), 1M global FCEVs. - Challenges: H₂ storage (350 bar), infra ($1M/station). - Memorability: FCEVs as “water-breathing cars,” exhaling only vapor. - Q128: Recently, the term "pumped-storage hydropower" is actually and appropriately discussed in the context of which one of the following? - Options: (a) Irrigation of terraced crop fields (b) Lift irrigation of cereal crops (c) Long duration energy storage (d) Rainwater harvesting system - Answer: (c) Long duration energy storage - Analysis: - Context and Significance: - Pumped-storage hydropower ($10B market, India 10%) stores 10 GWh (2025, CEA), balancing renewables (100 GW solar/wind). India’s 5 GW capacity (Koyna) supports net-zero goals (50% RE by 2030). - Explanation: - PSH pumps water to reservoirs (100 m head, 90% efficiency) during surplus (1 MW/m³/s), releasing for power (10 h storage, \( P \approx 9.8 \) MW/m³). - Why Not Other Options?: - (a) Incorrect: Terraced irrigation uses gravity, not PSH. - (b) Incorrect: Lift irrigation (pumps, 1 kW/ha) unrelated to storage. - (d) Incorrect: Rainwater harvesting (1 Mm³/year) is passive, not PSH. - Relevant Information: - Tech: Turbines (Francis, 95% efficiency), 100 MW/unit. Cost: $1M/MW. - Impact: 20% grid stability, 5% RE integration (India). - Challenges: Land (10 ha/MW), cost ($1B/plant). - Memorability: PSH as “energy battery,” storing power in elevated water.
